It was finally settled, however, that Wild Bill, a half-breed called Little Geary, and three other scouts should carry the dispatches, and they accordingly took their departure next day, with instructions to return to the command as soon as possible.
For several days we scouted along the Canadian River, but found no signs of Indians. General Carr then went back to his camp, and soon afterwards our wagon train came in from Fort Lyon with a fresh load of provisions. Our animals being in poor condition, we remained in different camps along San Francisco Creek and the north fork of the Canadian, until Wild Bill and his scouts returned from Camp Supply.
Among the scouts of Penrose’s command were fifteen Mexicans, and between them and the American scouts there had existed a feud; when General Carr took command of the expedition﻿—uniting it with his own﻿—and I was made chief of all the scouts, this feud grew more intense, and the Mexicans often threatened to clean us out; but they postponed the undertaking from time to time, until one day, while we were all at the sutler’s store, the long-expected fight took place, and resulted in the Mexicans getting severely beaten.
General Carr, upon hearing of the row, sent for Wild Bill and myself, he having concluded, from the various statements which had been made to him, that we were the instigators of the affair. But after listening to what we had to say, he thought that the Mexicans were as much to blame as we were.
It is not to be denied that Wild Bill and myself had been partaking too freely of “tanglefoot” that evening; and General Carr said to me: “Cody, there are plenty of antelopes in the country, and you can do some hunting for the camp while we stay here.”
“All right, General, I’ll do it.”
After that I put in my time hunting, and with splendid success, killing from fifteen to twenty antelopes a day, which kept the men well supplied with fresh meat.
At length, our horses and mules having become sufficiently recruited to travel, we returned to Fort Lyon, arriving there in March, 1869, where the command was to rest and recruit for thirty days, before proceeding to the Department of the Platte, whither it had been ordered.

===Chinese===

最终商定，由狂野比尔、一名绰号“小吉里”（Little Geary）的混血儿以及另外3名侦察兵负责押送公文；次日他们领受了尽速返营的训令后正式拔营启程。
接连好几天，我们沿着加拿大河两岸往返巡查侦察，却始终没有发现任何印第安人的敌情迹象。卡尔将军随后率部返回主力大营，不久之后，我们的大车辎重队载着满满当当的新鲜口粮从里昂堡班师归来。鉴于牲口体能极其孱弱疲惫，我们在旧金山溪与加拿大河北岔沿岸的各处营地轮番扎营休整，静候狂野比尔及其侦察小队从补给营完成使命凯旋。
在彭罗斯部下的侦察兵中有15名墨西哥人，他们与美国本土侦察兵之间长久以来积怨颇深、摩擦不断；当卡尔将军统领全军并将两军合编、委任我为全体侦察兵总指挥后，这种芥蒂敌意越发激化，墨西哥人屡次扬言要把我们彻底收拾干净；但他们雷声大雨点小一再推迟动手，直到有一天，当我们全员聚在军队随军小卖部（sutler’s store）喝酒时，蓄谋已久的恶斗终于彻底爆发，最终以墨西哥人被揍得落花流水鼻青脸肿告终。
卡尔将军听闻斗殴风声后，当即命人将狂野比尔和我传讯过去，从他听到的各方汇报来看，他认定我们俩正是挑起事端的始作俑者。但听完我们对事情原委的陈述与辩白后，将军认为墨西哥人同样难辞其咎。
无可否认的是，狂野比尔和我那天傍晚确实喝了太多烈酒“绊脚水”（tanglefoot）；卡尔将军对我正色道：“科迪，这片原野上有成群的叉角羚，趁大军在此休整期间，你还是多去给营里打点新鲜野味吧。”
“遵命，将军，包在我身上。”
自那之后，我把全副精力投入到了打猎当中，战果极其斐然，每天能猎杀15到20只叉角羚，确保了全营将士鲜肉供应充裕。
终于，战马与骡子的体魄得到了充分的休整复原，我们踏上归途班师返回里昂堡，于1869年3月抵达该哨所。按照预定调令，全军在此整饬休养30天后，即将开赴普拉特军区驻防履新。

## XII A Wedding / 第十二章 洞房花烛
===Original===

It was during the winter of 1864﻿–﻿65, while I was on detached service at military headquarters, at St. Louis, that I became acquainted with a young lady named Louisa Frederici, whom I greatly admired and in whose charming society I spent many a pleasant hour. The war closing in 1865, I was discharged, and after a brief visit at Leavenworth I returned to St. Louis, having made up my mind to capture the heart of Miss Frederici, whom I now adored above any other young lady I had ever seen. Her lovely face, her gentle disposition and her graceful manners won my admiration and love; and I was not slow in declaring my sentiments to her. The result was that I obtained her consent to marry me in the near future, and when I bade her goodbye I considered myself one of the happiest of men.
Meantime I drove a string of horses from Leavenworth to Fort Kearney, where I met my old friend Bill Trotter, who was then division stage agent. He employed me at once to drive stage between Kearney and Plum Creek, the road running near the spot where I had my first Indian fight with the McCarthy brothers, and where I killed my first Indian, nearly nine years before. I drove stage over this route until February, 1866, and while bounding over the cold, dreary road day after day, my thoughts turned continually towards my promised bride, until I at last determined to abandon staging forever, and marry and settle down. Immediately after coming to this conclusion, I went to St. Louis, where I was most cordially received by my sweetheart; it was arranged between us that our wedding should take place on the 6th day of March, following.
At last the day arrived, and the wedding ceremony was performed at the residence of the bride’s parents, in the presence of a large number of invited friends, whose hearty congratulations we received. I was certainly to be congratulated, for I had become possessed of a lovely and noble woman, and as I gazed upon her as she stood beside me arrayed in her wedding costume, I indeed felt proud of her; and from that time to this I have always thought that I made a most fortunate choice for a life partner.
An hour after the ceremony we﻿—my bride and myself﻿—were on board of a Missouri River steamboat, bound for our new home in Kansas. My wife’s parents had accompanied us to the boat, and had bidden us a fond farewell and a Godspeed on our journey.

===Chinese===

1864至1865年冬，当我在圣路易斯的军区司令部抽调执行特勤任务期间，结识了一位名叫路易莎·弗雷德里奇（Louisa Frederici）的年轻小姐。我深深为她倾倒，在她优雅迷人的陪伴下度过了无数温馨愉快的时光。1865年内战硝烟散尽，我退伍离开军队；在短暂回访莱文沃思后，我便马不停蹄地重返圣路易斯，一心要俘获弗雷德里奇小姐的芳心——此时此刻，在我见过的所有年轻女子中，唯有她令我魂牵梦萦。她秀丽绝伦的容颜、温柔贤淑的性情与优雅得体的仪态彻底征服了我的心，引得我心驰神往；我没有丝毫犹豫，很快向她吐露了肺腑爱意。令人欣慰的是，她欣然允诺在不久的将来与我喜结连理；在与她依依惜别时，我自视已是天底下最幸福的男人。
在此期间，我替人将一群马从莱文沃思赶运到卡尼堡（Fort Kearney），并在那里邂逅了我的老友比尔·特罗特（Bill Trotter），他当时正担任长途驿马车区间段调度主管。他当即雇佣我担任卡尼堡与梅溪（Plum Creek）之间的长途驿车车夫。这段路恰好途经当年我与麦卡锡兄弟（McCarthy brothers）打响平生第一场印第安遭遇战、并在近9年前亲手击毙第一个印第安人的旧地。我一直在这条线路上驾驭驿车，直至1866年2月；日复一日奔波在冰冷凄凉的荒野长道上，我的心绪时刻飞向我的未婚妻，最终我横下一条心，决意彻底告别驿车行当，成家立业过安稳日子。打定主意后，我立刻动身前往圣路易斯，受到了心上人无比热烈深情的迎接；我们商定，婚期定在随后的3月6日。
吉日终于来临，婚礼在新娘父母的府邸隆重举行，众多受邀前来的亲朋好友齐聚一堂，纷纷向我们致以最热烈诚挚的祝福。我确实值得所有人羡慕与道贺，因为我迎娶了一位秀外慧中、品格高尚的佳人；当我凝视着身披盛装婚纱肃立在我身侧的新娘时，内心充满了无尽的骄傲与自豪；自那一刻起直至今日，我始终认定这是我人生旅途中所做出的最无比幸运的良缘抉择。
婚礼仪式结束仅一个小时后，我们新婚夫妇便登上了航行在密苏里河上的明轮蒸汽客轮，启程奔赴我们在堪萨斯的新家。岳父岳母一路送我们来到码头登船，依依惜别，衷心祈愿我们前程顺遂平安。

===Original===

During the trip up the river several very amusing, yet awkward incidents occurred, some of which I cannot resist relating. There happened to be on board the boat an excursion party from Lexington, Missouri, and those comprising it seemed to shun me, for some reason which I could not then account for. They would point at me, and quietly talk among themselves, and eye me very closely. Their actions seemed very strange to me. After the boat had proceeded some little distance, I made the acquaintance of several families from Indiana, who were en route to Kansas. A gentleman, who seemed to be the leader of these colonists, said to me, “The people of this excursion party don’t seem to have any great love for you.”
“What does it mean?” I asked; “What are they saying? It’s all a mystery to me.”
“They say that you are one of the Kansas jay-hawkers, and one of Jennison’s house burners,” replied the gentleman.
“I am from Kansas﻿—that’s true; and was a soldier and a scout in the Union army,” said I; “and I was in Kansas during the border ruffian war of 1856. Perhaps these people know who I am, and that explains their hard looks.” I had a lengthy conversation with this gentleman﻿—for such he seemed to be﻿—and entertained him with several chapters of the history of the early Kansas troubles, and told him the experiences of my own family.
In the evening the Lexington folks got up a dance, but neither the Indiana people, my wife or myself were invited to join them. My newfound friend thereupon came to me and said: “Mr. Cody, let us have a dance of our own.”
“Very well,” was my reply.
“We have some musicians along with us, so we can have plenty of music,” remarked the gentleman.
“Good enough!” said I, “and I will hire the negro barber to play the violin for us. He is a good fiddler, as I heard him playing only a little while ago.” The result was that we soon organized a good string band and had a splendid dance, keeping it up as long as the Lexington party did theirs.
The second day out from St. Louis, the boat stopped to wood up, at a wild-looking landing. Suddenly twenty horsemen were seen galloping up through the timber, and as they came nearer the boat they fired on the negro deckhands, against whom they seemed to have a special grudge, and who were engaged in throwing wood on board.

===Chinese===

溯流而上的航程中发生过几桩啼笑皆非却又颇为尴尬的趣事轶闻，令我忍不住在此一叙。客轮上恰好搭乘着一个来自密苏里州列克星敦（Lexington）的观光旅游团，这群人不知何故似乎刻意对我避之唯恐不及。他们时不时对着我指指点点、交头接耳窃窃私语，还拿异样的警惕眼神上上下下打量我。他们的古怪举动让我感到丈二和尚摸不着头脑。客轮航行了一段距离后，我结识了同船的几户正举家迁往堪萨斯的印第安纳州移民家庭。那位看似移民领头人的绅士凑到我跟前低声说道：“那帮列克星敦来的观光客，对你似乎怀着不小的敌意啊。”
“这是何缘故？”我纳闷地问，“他们都在嘀咕些什么？我实在想不通。”
“他们私下里说你是堪萨斯的‘兜头鸟掠夺者’（jay-hawkers），还是当年詹尼森纵火烧房屠夫帮里的帮凶。”那位绅士回答道。
“我是堪萨斯人——这不假；而且我也确实曾是联邦正规军里的士兵与侦察兵，”我说，“在1856年堪萨斯边疆匪帮混战流血冲突期间，我正好身在堪萨斯。或许这群人认出了我的底细，这大概就是他们对我横眉冷对的原因吧。”我与这位文质彬彬的绅士长谈了一番，为他讲述了早期堪萨斯流血动乱历史的生动章节，并向他倾诉了我们自己家庭在那场劫难中的惨痛遭遇。
到了傍晚，列克星敦那一伙人在甲板上组织了一场舞会，但既没有邀请印第安纳的移民，也没有邀请我和我的妻子。我那位新结识的朋友随即找上门来提议：“科迪先生，咱们也自己办一场舞会热闹热闹如何？”
“太好了，赞成！”我爽快地答道。
“我们车队里正好带了几名乐手，伴奏乐曲管够。”绅士说道。
“那再好不过了！”我说，“我再去把船上的黑人理发师请过来给咱们拉小提琴。刚才我听他拉过一曲，琴艺相当地道。”果不其然，我们很快组建了一支实力强劲的弦乐队，热热闹闹地开起了一场盛大的舞会，欢声笑语彻夜狂欢，尽兴程度丝毫不亚于列克星敦那一帮人。
从圣路易斯启航的第二天，客轮在一个荒凉险僻的小码头停靠加装木柴燃料。突然间，只见20名全副武装的骑手纵马穿过树林疾驰而来；当他们逼近客轮时，竟然举枪朝着正在甲板与码头间往船上搬运木柴的黑人水手猛烈扫射——他们似乎对黑人士兵和水手怀有刻骨铭心的仇恨。

===Original===

The negroes all quickly jumped on the boat and pulled in the gang plank, and the captain had only just time to get the steamer out into the stream before the bushwhackers﻿—for such they proved to be﻿—appeared on the bank.
“Where is the black abolition jay-hawker?” shouted the leader.
“Show him to us, and we’ll shoot him,” yelled another.
But as the boat had got well out in the river by this time, they could not board us, and the captain ordering a full head of steam, pulled out and left them.
I afterwards ascertained that some of the Missourians, who were with the excursion party, were bushwhackers themselves, and had telegraphed to their friends from some previous landing that I was on board, telling them to come to the landing which we had just left, and take me off. Had the villains captured me they would have undoubtedly put an end to my career, and the public would never have had the pleasure of being bored by this autobiography.
I noticed that my wife felt grieved over the manner in which these people had treated me. Just married, she was going into a new country, and seeing how her husband was regarded, how he had been shunned, and how his life had been threatened, I was afraid she might come to the conclusion too soon that she had wedded a “hard customer.” So when the boat landed at Kansas City I telegraphed to some of my friends in Leavenworth that I would arrive there in the evening. My object was to have my acquaintances give me a reception, so that my wife could see that I really did have some friends, and was not so bad a man as the bushwhackers tried to make out.
Just as I expected, when the boat reached Leavenworth, I found a general roundup of friends at the landing to receive us. There were about sixty gentlemen and ladies. They had a band of music with them, and we were given a fine serenade. Taking carriages, we all drove to South Leavenworth to the home of my sister Eliza, who had married George Myers, and there we were given a very handsome reception. All this cheered up my wife, who concluded that I was not a desperado after all.
Having promised my wife that I would abandon the plains, I rented a hotel in Salt Creek Valley﻿—the same house by the way, which my mother had formerly kept, but which was then owned by Dr. J. J. Crook, late surgeon of the 7th Kansas.

===Chinese===

黑人水手们吓得纷纷连滚带爬跳回船上，猛力收回登船跳板；船长千钧一发之际加大马力将轮船调头驶离岸边，而此时丛林游击队（bushwhackers）——事实证明这帮亡命徒正是南军游击队残匪——已然杀到了河岸码头前。
“那个黑鬼废奴派的兜头鸟杂种缩在哪儿？”头目厉声狂吼道。
“把他揪出来，看老子不一枪崩了他！”另一个狂徒歇斯底里地咆哮。
但此时轮船早已脱离岸滩滑入宽阔江心，他们根本无法强行登船；船长下令全速加压蒸汽，轮船喷着滚滚浓烟斩浪而去，将这帮狂徒远远甩在了身后。
事后我才查明，同船列克星敦观光团里的几名密苏里人本身就是这帮丛林游击队的眼线党羽；他们早在前一个停靠港便给岸上的同伙拍发电报，告密说我身在这艘船上，指使他们在我们刚刚离开的那个僻静码头设伏拦截，企图将我强行拖下船处决。若是落入这帮恶徒的毒手，我的生命与边疆生涯必定当场画上句号，而诸位读者今天也断无可能再有幸“忍受”这本自传的唠叨了。
我注意到妻子因这帮人对我的仇视敌意而忧心忡忡、暗自神伤。她刚刚新婚燕尔，随我远赴陌生的荒蛮边疆，冷不防亲眼目睹丈夫在当地竟如此遭人白眼冷落、甚至随时面临杀身之祸，我生怕她会草率地认定自己错嫁给了一个“亡命凶徒”。因此，当轮船在堪萨斯城（Kansas City）靠岸时，我立刻向莱文沃思的好友们发出一封加急电报，告知他们我们将在当晚抵运。我的用意是让昔日的朋友们为我们举办一场隆重的欢迎仪式，好让妻子亲眼看到，我在家乡不仅朋友遍天下，而且绝非丛林暴徒所肆意抹黑的那种十恶不赦之徒。
正如我所料，当轮船靠上莱文沃思码头时，码头上早已黑压压聚集了一大群专程前来迎接我们的挚友故旧。总共来了约60位体面的绅士与名媛，他们甚至带来了一整支军乐队，在码头为我们演奏了一场动听悦耳的迎宾小夜曲。大伙分别登上马车，一路浩浩荡荡驶向南莱文沃思我姐姐伊丽莎（Eliza）的家——她嫁给了乔治·迈尔斯（George Myers）——并在那里为我们举办了一场隆重华丽的迎宾晚宴。眼前这热闹非凡的一幕彻底驱散了妻子的阴霾愁绪，让她欣慰地确信，自己的丈夫终究不是个无恶不作的亡命之徒。
由于此前我曾向妻子郑重许诺，成家后绝不再漂泊荒原打打杀杀，我便在盐溪谷（Salt Creek Valley）租下了一处旅店——顺便提一句，这栋房子正是当年我母亲经营过的旧宅，此时产权已归属于堪萨斯第七团的前军医J·J·克鲁克医生（Dr. J. J. Crook）。

===Original===

This hotel I called the Golden Rule House, and I kept it until the next September. People generally said I made a good landlord, and knew how to run a hotel﻿—a business qualification which, it is said, is possessed by comparatively few men. But it proved too tame employment for me, and again I sighed for the freedom of the plains. Believing that I could make more money out West on the frontier than I could at Salt Creek Valley, I sold out the Golden Rule House, and started alone for Saline, Kansas, which was then the end of the track of the Kansas Pacific railway, which was at that time being built across the plains. On my way I stopped at Junction City, where I again met my old friend Wild Bill, who was scouting for the government; his headquarters being at Fort Ellsworth, afterwards called Fort Harker. He told me that they needed more scouts at this post, and I accordingly accompanied him to that fort, where I had no difficulty in obtaining employment.
During the winter of 1866﻿–﻿67, I scouted between Fort Ellsworth and Fort Fletcher. In the spring of 1867 I was at Fort Fletcher, when General Custer came out to go on an Indian expedition with General Hancock. I remained at this post until it was drowned out by the heavy floods of Big Creek, on which it was located; the water rose about the fortifications and rendered the place unfit for occupancy; so the government abandoned the fort, and moved the troops and supplies to a new post﻿—which had been named Fort Hays﻿—located further west, on the south fork of Big Creek. It was while scouting in the vicinity of Fort Hays that I had my first ride with the dashing and gallant Custer, who had come up to the post from Fort Ellsworth with an escort of only ten men. He wanted a guide to pilot him to Fort Larned, a distance of sixty-five miles across the country.
I was ordered by the commanding officer to guide General Custer to his desired destination, and I soon received word from the General that he would start out in the morning with the intention of making the trip in one day. Early in the morning, after a good night’s rest, I was on hand, mounted on my large mouse-colored mule﻿—an animal of great endurance﻿—and ready for the journey; when the General saw me, he said:
“Cody, I want to travel fast and go through as quickly as possible, and I don’t think that mule of yours is fast enough to suit me.”

===Chinese===

我将这座客栈命名为“黄金法则之家”（Golden Rule House），一直经营打理到次年9月。来往过客普遍夸奖我是个称职周到的大掌柜，精通经营客栈的门道——据说能掌握这门生意的男人在世上堪称凤毛麟角。但这等平淡安稳的琐碎营生对我而言未免过于索然无味，不久我又开始深深怀念起大草原上纵马狂奔的自由天地。深信自己在西部蛮荒边疆定能比困守盐溪谷赚到多得多的真金白银，我索性将黄金法则之家盘了出去，孤身一人启程奔赴堪萨斯的萨林（Saline）——那里正是彼时跨越大平原修筑的堪萨斯太平洋铁路的轨道铺设终点站。途经章克申城（Junction City）时，我再度邂逅了我的老战友狂野比尔，他当时正受雇为政府担任特遣侦察兵，其驻防大本营设在埃尔斯沃思堡（Fort Ellsworth，后改称哈克堡）。他告诉我该要塞目前急需大批精锐侦察兵，我便随他一同前往该堡，顺理成章地在那里领受了一份侦察差事。
1866至1867年冬，我往返巡弋于埃尔斯沃思堡与弗莱彻堡（Fort Fletcher）之间执行前哨侦察。1867年春，我正驻守弗莱彻堡，恰逢卡斯特将军（General Custer）亲临边疆，准备与汉考克将军（General Hancock）一道出征征讨印第安人。我一直留守在该哨所，直到要塞依傍的比格溪爆发山洪暴发将其彻底淹没；汹涌的洪水淹没了防御工事，使该地无法再行驻军；于是联邦政府废弃了这座要塞，将驻军与物资转移到了西面位于比格溪南岔的一处新建哨所——即后来赫赫大名的海斯堡（Fort Hays）。正是在海斯堡周边执行侦察任务期间，我平生第一次与那位英姿飒爽、骁勇绝伦的卡斯特将军并肩策马驰骋。他当时仅仅带着10名随从骑兵从埃尔斯沃思堡轻装赶来，急需一名熟悉地形的老练向导护送他横穿荒原前往65英里外的拉尼德堡（Fort Larned）。
我接到了驻军指挥官的严令，担任卡斯特将军的专属向导；将军很快传话给我，说他打算清晨拂晓便启程拔营，计划用一天时间一日之内强行军跑完这65英里全程。经过一夜酣畅休憩，清晨天刚大亮我便依令守候在营门前，跨骑着我那匹体格硕大、呈鼠灰色的神勇役骡——这是一头脚力和耐力极其惊人的神兽；将军一见到我的坐骑，不由得皱起眉头说道：
“科迪，这趟路我要全速行军、尽最快速度赶到，我看你胯下这头笨骡子恐怕跑得太慢，跟不上我的节拍。”

===Original===

“General, never mind the mule,” said I, “he’ll get there as soon as your horses. That mule is a good one,” as I knew that the animal was better than most horses.
“Very well; go ahead, then,” said he, though he looked as if he thought I would delay the party on the road.
For the first fifteen miles, until we came to the Smoky Hill River, which we were to cross, I could hardly keep the mule in advance of the General, who rode a frisky, impatient and ambitious thoroughbred steed; in fact, the whole party was finely mounted. The General repeatedly told me that the mule was “no good,” and that I ought to have had a good horse. But after crossing the river and striking the sand-hills, I began letting my mule out a little, and putting the “persuaders” to him. He was soon out-traveling the horses, and by the time we had made about half the distance to Fort Larned, I occasionally had to wait for the General or some of his party, as their horses were beginning to show signs of fatigue.
“General, how about this mule, anyhow?” I asked, at last.
“Cody, you have a better vehicle than I thought you had,” was his reply.
From that time on to Fort Larned I had no trouble in keeping ahead of the party. We rode into the fort at four o’clock in the afternoon with about half the escort only, the rest having lagged far behind.
General Custer thanked me for having brought him straight across the country without any trail, and said that if I were not engaged as post scout at Fort Hays he would like to have me accompany him as one of his scouts during the summer; and he added that whenever I was out of employment, if I would come to him he would find something for me to do. This was the beginning of my acquaintance with General Custer, whom I always admired as a man and as an officer.
A few days after my return to Fort Hays, the Indians made a raid on the Kansas Pacific Railroad, killing five or six men and running off about one hundred horses and mules. The news was brought to the commanding officer, who immediately ordered Major Arms, of the Tenth Cavalry﻿—which, by the way, was a negro regiment﻿—with his company and one mountain howitzer, to go in pursuit of the redskins, and I was sent along with the expedition as scout and guide.

===Chinese===

“将军，您大可不必为这头骡子操心，”我说，“您的战马多快赶到，它就能多快赶到。这可是一头千里挑一的神骡。”因为我心里比谁都清楚，这头牲口的脚力耐力远胜过绝大多数普通战马。
“那好，前头带路吧。”将军说道，但脸上的神色分明写着生怕我会在半路上拖累全队的行程。
在渡过斯莫基希尔河（Smoky Hill River）之前的前15英里路程中，由于将军胯下骑着一匹精力旺盛、急躁好胜的纯种纯血宝马，而且全队扈从骑兵个个坐骑精良，我确实很难让骡子始终跑在将军前面。将军一路上不时冲我嚷嚷，说这头骡子“根本不顶事”，我真该换一匹正经的快马。然而，一旦渡过河流踏上无边无际的起伏沙丘，我便开始稍稍放宽缰绳，动用马刺给这头神骡来上几记“催化剂”。没过多久，它的耐力便在深沙中彻底压倒了战马群；等我们跑完通往拉尼德堡的大约一半路程时，我甚至时不时得勒住缰绳驻足等候将军和他的随从，因为他们的战马早已呼哧带喘显露疲态。
“将军，您现在瞧着我这头骡子究竟如何啊？”最终我忍不住打趣地问道。
“科迪，你这脚力工具比我想象的可强出太多了。”他心悦诚服地叹道。
自那一刻起直抵拉尼德堡，我一路遥遥领先在前领跑毫无压力。当天下午4点钟整，我们策马冲进了拉尼德堡的大门，而随行的护卫骑兵只有大约一半勉强跟了上来，其余人马早就被远远甩在了身后。
卡斯特将军对我感激不尽，赞赏我在毫无现成车马道可循的苍茫荒原上，竟能领着他笔直穿插一天抵达目的地；他表示如果我没有受雇担任海斯堡的常驻侦察兵，他极希望在整个夏季雇佣我作为他的随军侦察兵；他还亲切地补充说，只要我哪天没有差事在身，随时可以去找他，他定会为我安排体面差事。这便是我与卡斯特将军深厚交情的开端，在为人与统兵两方面，我对他生平始终怀有无上的崇敬爱戴。
返回海斯堡数日之后，印第安人突袭了堪萨斯太平洋铁路工地，打死了五六名工人，并卷走了大约100匹战马与骡子。急报送达驻军指挥官案头，长官当即责令第十骑兵团——顺便说一句，这是一支清一色的黑人士兵兵团——的阿姆斯少校（Major Arms）率领其骑兵连，配属一门山地榴弹炮（mountain howitzer），火速开拔追剿印第安匪徒；我则奉命随军出征，担任特遣侦察兵兼向导。

===Original===

On the second day out we suddenly discovered, on the opposite side of the Saline River, about a mile distant, a large body of Indians, who were charging down upon us. Major Arms, placing the cannon on a little knoll, limbered it up and left twenty men to guard it; and then, with the rest of the command, he crossed the river to meet the Indians.
Just as he had got the men over the stream, we heard a terrific yelling and shouting in our rear, and looking back to the knoll where the cannon had been stationed, we saw the negroes, who had been left there to guard the gun, flying towards us, being pursued by about one hundred Indians; while another large party of the latter were dancing around the captured cannon, as if they had got hold of an elephant and did not know what to do with it.
Major Arms turned his command back and drove the Indians from the gun. The troops then dismounted and took position there. Quite a severe fight ensued, lasting about two hours. Five or six of the soldiers, as well as Major Arms, were wounded, and several of the horses were shot. The Indians seemed to grow thicker and thicker, as if receiving reinforcements from some large party. The colored troops, who had been bragging all the way that if they could only see some Indians “dey would blow ’em off de farm,”﻿—which was a favorite expression of theirs﻿—were now singing a different tune. Every time the Indians would make a charge at us, the darkeys would cry out:
“Heah dey cum;” “Dey must be ten thousand ob ’em;” “De whole country is alive wid ’em;” “Massa Bill, does you tink we is eber agoin’ to get out o’ heah?” and many other similar expressions.
Major Arms, who was wounded and lying under the cannon﻿—which, by the way, had become useless﻿—called me up and asked if I thought there was any show of getting back to the fort. I replied that there was.
Orders were accordingly given by Major Arms for a retreat, the cannon being left behind. During the movement several of our men were killed, but as night came and dense darkness prevailed, we succeeded in making good headway, and got into Fort Hays just at daylight next morning, in a very played-out condition.
During our absence the cholera had broken out at the post, and five or six men were dying daily.

===Chinese===

出征的第二天，我们猛然望见萨林河对岸大约1英里开外，大批印第安人正如同潮水般朝我们狂奔冲杀而来。阿姆斯少校当即下令将大炮架设在路旁的一处低矮小圆丘上，卸下前车架好火炮，并留下20名黑人士兵负责看守掩护；随后，他率领其余主力人马涉水渡过河流，正面迎击印第安人。
就在少校刚刚率队渡过河心之际，我们身后突然爆发出震耳欲聋的惊恐尖叫与嘶喊声；回过头朝架设大炮的小圆丘望去，只见刚才留守看管火炮的黑人士兵正丢盔弃甲朝着我们飞奔溃逃，身后紧追着大约100名杀气腾腾的印第安人；而另一大帮印第安人则正围着缴获的大炮载歌载舞欢呼雀跃，那滑稽模样仿佛一群突然捕获了一头大象却全然不知该如何下手的生番。
阿姆斯少校当即勒马回师，猛扑回去驱散了炮位周围的印第安人。官兵们随即翻身下马，依托炮位依托阵地展开就地防御。一场极其激烈的恶战随即打响，整整持续了约两个小时。包括阿姆斯少校在内的五六名官兵负了伤，数匹战马中弹倒毙。印第安人越聚越多，仿佛正源源不断得到大批增援部队的生力补充。此前一路上吹嘘个不停、扬言只要一见到印第安人“非得把他们轰出地皮不可”——这是黑人士兵的一句口头禅——的这帮弟兄们，此刻腔调早已全变了样。每当印第安人发起一轮疯狂冲锋，黑人士兵便吓得失声尖叫：
“他们杀上来啦！”“敌军少说也有一万大军啊！”“漫山遍野全都是他们的人马啊！”“比尔大爷，您瞧咱们这回还能活着逃出去吗？”诸如此类惊恐绝望的喊叫不绝于耳。
负伤倒在已然损坏失效的大炮底下的阿姆斯少校把我叫到身边，急促地询问我究竟还有没有把握杀出重围撤回要塞。我斩钉截铁地回答说绝对有希望。
阿姆斯少校随即忍痛下达撤退命令，将笨重的大炮遗弃在阵地上。在突围撤退途中，又有几名战友不幸中弹阵亡；但随着夜幕降临、漆黑的浓雾遮蔽了一切，我们得以全速穿插撤离，最终在次日清晨破晓时分，全军拖着极度疲惫瘫软的身躯狼狈撤回了海斯堡。
而在我们随军出征期间，海斯堡哨所内霍乱暴发，每天都有五六名官兵不幸病故。

===Original===

It was difficult to tell which was the greater danger﻿—fighting Indians on the prairie, or facing the cholera in camp; but the former was decidedly the more inviting.

===Chinese===

此时此刻，委实难以断定究竟哪一种凶险更为致命——是在茫茫荒原上与印第安人血战厮杀，还是在军营帐篷里直面霍乱瘟疫的吞噬；但两相权衡，前者显然更具吸引力，也更让人心甘情愿去面对。

## VII Accidents and Escapes / 第七章 意外与脱险
===Original===

My restless, roaming spirit would not allow me to remain at home very long, and in November, after the recovery of my mother, I went up the Republican River and its tributaries on a trapping expedition in company with Dave Harrington. Our outfit consisted of one wagon and a yoke of oxen for the transportation of provisions, traps, and other necessaries. We began trapping near Junction City, Kansas, and then proceeded up the Republican River to the mouth of Prairie Dog Creek, where we found plenty of beavers.
Having seen no signs of Indians thus far, we felt comparatively safe. We were catching a large number of beavers and were prospering finely, when one of our oxen, having become rather poor, slipped and fell upon the ice, dislocating his hip, so that we had to shoot him to end his misery. This left us without a team; but we cared little for that, however, as we had made up our minds to remain there till spring, when, and it was decided, that one of us should go to the nearest settlement and get a yoke of oxen with which to haul our wagon into some place of safety where we could leave it.
We would probably have pulled through the winter all right had it not been for a very serious accident which befell me just at that time. Spying a herd of elk, we started in pursuit of them, and creeping up towards them as slyly as possible, while going around the bend of a sharp bluff or bank of the creek I slipped and broke my leg just above the ankle. Notwithstanding the great pain I was suffering, Harrington could not help laughing when I urged him to shoot me, as he had the ox, and thus end my misery. He told me to “brace up,” and that he would bring me out “all right.” “I am not much of a surgeon,” said he, “but I can fix that leg of yours, even if I haven’t got a diploma.”
He succeeded in getting me back to camp, which was only a few yards from the creek, and then he set the fracture as well as he knew how, and made me as comfortable as was possible under the circumstances. We then discussed the situation, which to say the least, looked pretty blue. Knowing that, owing to our mishaps, we could not do anything more that winter, and as I dreaded the idea of lying there on my back with a broken leg for weeks, and perhaps months, I prevailed upon Harrington to go the nearest settlement﻿—about 125 miles distant﻿—to obtain a yoke of cattle, and then come back for me.

===Chinese===

我天生那种躁动不安、四海为家的浪子心性，绝不容许我在家里安分守己地长久呆着。11月里，待母亲身体稍有康复，我便与戴夫·哈灵顿（Dave Harrington）结伴，沿着里帕布利肯河及其支流溯流而上，展开了一场捕兽狩猎远征。我们的行头包括一辆大车和一对用于运输口粮、捕兽夹以及其他必需品的挽牛。我们在堪萨斯州的章克申城（Junction City）附近拉开捕兽序幕，随后沿着里帕布利肯河一路推进到草原犬溪（Prairie Dog Creek）河口，在那里我们发现了数不胜数的河狸。
沿途一直未见印第安人的敌情迹象，我们自觉得格外安全。我们捕获了大量河狸，狩猎收益蒸蒸日上。然而天有不测风云，我们的一头挽牛由于日益瘦弱，在冰面上滑倒摔断了胯骨，我们不得不开枪将它打死以解除它的痛苦。这使我们失去了拉车的一套牲口；但我们对此并没太放在心上，反正我们早已打定主意要在荒原驻留到次年开春；到那时，商定由我们中的一人徒步前往最近的拓荒定居点，牵回一对挽牛把大车拉到某处安全的庇护所妥善安放。
若非就在此时一场严重的飞来横祸降临到我头上，我们本可以平平安安熬过这个寒冬。当时我们窥见了一群麋鹿，便悄无声息地摸上去展开追猎；就在我们尽可能诡秘地匍匐接近、正绕过溪流岸边一处陡峭悬崖弯道的当口，我脚下一滑狠狠摔倒在地，脚踝正上方的腿骨当场咔嚓折断。尽管我痛得撕心裂肺、死去活来，但当我苦苦哀求哈灵顿像打死那头伤牛一样朝我开上一枪、好帮我彻底解脱痛苦时，他还是忍不住笑出声来。他叫我“咬紧牙关撑住”，夸口说自己定能让我“完好如初”。“我算不上什么外科名医，”他说，“但就算我没揣着行医文凭，收拾你这根断腿也是小菜一碟。”
他设法把我背回了距离溪流仅数码之遥的营地，随后倾其所能帮我接上了骨折断骨，并在当时的恶劣条件下尽可能让我躺得舒适些。接着我们商讨起眼下的危局，说句老实话，前景看起来可谓暗无天日。深知横遭此祸后这个冬天再也干不成任何捕兽营生，一想到自己要断着腿直挺挺仰面躺在这里受罪数周、乃至数月，我便极力劝说哈灵顿立刻动身徒步赶往最近的定居点——相距约125英里之遥——去借上一对耕牛，然后再赶回来接我脱离苦海。

===Original===

This he consented to do; but before leaving he gathered plenty of wood, and as the ground was covered with snow, I would have no difficulty in getting water if I had a fire. There was plenty of fresh meat and other provisions in the “dugout,” so that I had no fears of starvation. The “dugout,” which we had built immediately after we had determined to remain there all winter, was a very cosy hole in the ground, covered with poles, grass and sod, with a fireplace in one end.
Harrington thought it would take him twenty days or more to make the round trip; but being well provided for﻿—for this length of time﻿—I urged him to go at once. Bidding me goodbye he started on foot. After his departure, each day, as it came and went, seemed to grow longer to me as I lay there helpless and alone. I made a note of each day, so as to know the time when I might expect him back.
On the twelfth day after Harrington left me, I was awakened from a sound sleep by someone touching me upon the shoulder. I looked up and was astonished to see an Indian warrior standing at my side. His face was hideously daubed with paint, which told me more forcibly than words could have done that he was on the warpath. He spoke to me in broken English and Sioux mixed, and I understood him to ask what I was doing there, and how many there were with me.
By this time the little dugout was nearly filled with other Indians, who had been peeping in at the door, and I could hear voices of still more outside as well as the stamping of horses. I began to think that my time had come, as the saying is, when into the cabin stepped an elderly Indian, whom I readily recognized as old Rain-in-the-Face, a Sioux chief from the vicinity of Fort Laramie. I rose up as well as I could and showed him my broken leg. I told him where I had seen him, and asked him if he remembered me. He replied that he knew me well, and that I used to come to his lodge at Fort Laramie to visit him. I then managed to make him understand that I was there alone and having broken my leg, I had sent my partner off for a team to take me away. I asked him if his young men intended to kill me, and he answered, that was what they had proposed to do, but he would see what they had to say.

===Chinese===

他最终点头应承了下来；但在动身启程之前，他替我捡拾并储备了堆积如山的干柴。由于大地覆盖着皑皑厚雪，只要身边有火，我便不难融雪取水。这间“地窨子”（dugout）里储备着充足的鲜肉和其他干粮，因此短时间内我丝毫不用担心挨饿。这间地窨子是我们决定在此长留越冬后立刻动手搭建的，是在地下挖出的一处极舒适的小地穴，顶部覆盖着圆木杆、干草与草皮泥炭，地穴一端还砌了一座生火取暖的壁炉。
哈灵顿估计自己打个来回至少需要20天或更长时间；但鉴于这段时间内的生活给养储备充足，我催促他务必尽早动身。与我互道珍重作别后，他只身一人徒步踏上了茫茫征途。在他离去之后，我孤立无援、动弹不得地孤身瘫卧在穴中，只觉得每一个日出日落的漫长白天都显得越发度日如年。我在墙壁上仔细刻划下每一天的标记，以便心中有数，知道究竟何时才能盼到他接应归来。
就在哈灵顿离开后的第12天，沉睡中的我猛然被人轻拍肩膀惊醒。我睁眼抬头一瞧，赫然惊骇地发现一名印第安勇士正肃立在我的身旁。他的脸上涂抹着狰狞骇人的油彩，这副战妆比任何言语都更直白有力地昭告着：他正处于嗜血厮杀的征战状态！他用夹杂着苏族语的蹩脚英语冲我发话，我勉强听懂他是在质问我在这里干什么，以及还有多少同伙藏在附近。
转眼之间，狭小的地窨子几乎被挤得水泄不通，门外探头探脑张望着更多印第安人，我甚至能听见外头更多嘈杂的喧嚣人声与战马踏雪的蹄声。正如老话所说，我心想自己的大限这回恐怕真的是到了。就在这命悬一线的节骨眼上，一位上了年纪的印第安老者迈步踱进了地穴，我定睛一瞧，一眼便认出他不是别人，正是拉勒米堡（Fort Laramie）附近的苏族老酋长“脸上落雨”（Rain-in-the-Face）。我挣扎着强撑起身子，向他展示我那条折断的断腿。我向他提起曾在何处与他相识，问他是否还认得我。他回答说他当然认得我，记得我当年常常跑到拉勒米堡他的帐篷里去探望他。我随即设法让他明白，我孤身一人滞留在此，因为摔断了腿，已经打发同伴去远方找大车来接我脱险。我颤声询问他的年轻勇士们是否打算要我的命，他直言不讳地回答说，年轻人确实正是这么打算的，不过他会出面听听大家的意见。

===Original===

The Indians then talked among themselves for a few minutes, and upon the conclusion of the consultation, old Rain-in-the-Face turned to me and gave me to understand that as I was yet a “papoose,” or a very young man, they would not take my life. But one of his men, who had no firearms, wanted my gun and pistol. I implored old Rain-in-the-Face to be allowed to keep the weapons, or at least one of them, as I needed something with which to keep the wolves away. He replied that as his young men were out on the war path, he had induced them to spare my life; but he could not prevent them from taking what ever else they wanted.
They unsaddled their horses as if to remain there for some time, and sure enough they stayed the remainder of the day and all night. They built a fire in the dugout and cooked a lot of my provisions, helping themselves to everything as if they owned it. However, they were polite enough to give me some of the food after they had cooked it. It was a sumptuous feast that they had, and they seemed to relish it as if it was the best layout they had had for many a long day. They took all my sugar and coffee, and left me only some meat and a small quantity of flour, a little salt and some baking powder. They also robbed me of such cooking utensils as they wished; then bidding me goodbye, early in the morning, they mounted their ponies and rode off to the south, evidently bent on some murdering and thieving expedition.
I was glad enough to see them leave, as my life had undoubtedly hung by a thread during their presence. I am confident that had it not been for my youth and the timely recognition and interference of old Rain-in-the-Face they would have killed me without any hesitation or ceremony.
The second day after they had gone it began snowing, and for three long and weary days the snow continued to fall thick and fast. It blocked the doorway and covered the dugout to the depth of several feet, so that I became a snowbound prisoner. My wood was mostly under the snow, and it was with great difficulty that I could get enough to start a fire with. My prospects were gloomy indeed. I had just faced death at the hands of the Indians, and now I was in danger of losing my life from starvation and cold.

===Chinese===

印第安人聚在一起交头接耳密商了几分钟；密商结束后，老“脸上落雨”转过身来向我示意：鉴于我眼下还只是个“毛头小崽子”（papoose）、一个涉世未深的年轻小伙，他们决定网开一面饶我不死。但他手下有一名没有火器的年轻战士，非要强夺我的步枪与手枪。我苦苦哀求老酋长准许我保留防身武器，哪怕只留下一支也好，因为在这荒郊野岭我急需用枪驱赶狼群。老酋长叹息着答复说，由于这帮年轻勇士正在征战杀戮途中，他好说歹说才劝阻他们饶下了我的性命；但他实在无法阻拦他们搜刮他们看中的任何财物战利品。
他们纷纷卸下马鞍，俨然打算在此常驻歇脚，果不其然，他们在营地里耗完了大半天，随后整整宿营了一夜。他们在我的地窨子里生起大火，大肆烹煮我的存粮，理直气壮地享用着我的一切物资，仿佛他们才是这间宅子的主人。不过，他们倒还算客气体面，煮好食物后也慷慨地分给了我一份。那是一顿极丰盛的大餐，他们狼吞虎咽津津有味，似乎这是他们漫长岁月以来享用过的最上乘的一顿筵席。他们席卷走了我全部的白糖与咖啡，仅仅留给我少许肉食、一小口袋面粉、一点食盐以及少许发酵粉。他们还顺手牵羊掠夺走了几样他们相中的炊具；随后，清晨一大早，他们向我拱手告别，翻身跃上马背一路向南绝尘而去，显而易见又是去赶赴某场烧杀劫掠的罪恶远征了。
目送他们远去，我心头一块巨石总算落了地，因为他们在场期间，我的身家性命委实悬于一线。我深信若非看在我年少轻狂的份上，加上老“脸上落雨”的及时相认与出面干预，他们早就不假思索毫不客气地将我一刀抹了脖子。
在他们离去的第二天，天公不作美下起了鹅毛大雪，整整漫长煎熬的三天三夜，暴雪铺天盖地狂泻不止。积雪彻底封死了地穴入口，并将整座地窨子掩埋了数英尺之深，使我彻底沦为了一名被大雪围困的囚徒。我的干柴绝大多数被深埋在雪堆之下，要想扒拉出足够生火的木料变得极度艰难。我的前景变得暗无天日：刚刚在印第安人的屠刀下侥幸死里逃生，此刻却再度面临在刺骨严寒与绝粮饥荒中被活活冻死饿死的灭顶之灾。

===Original===

I knew that the heavy snow would surely delay Harrington on his return; and I feared that he might have perished in the storm, or that some other accident might have befallen him. Perhaps some wandering band of Indians had run across him and killed him.
I was continually thinking of all these possibilities, and I must say that my outlook seemed desperate. At last the twentieth day arrived﻿—the day on which Harrington was to return﻿—and I counted the hours from morning till night, but the day passed away with no signs of Harrington. The wolves made the night hideous with their howls; they gathered around the dugout; ran over the roof; and pawed and scratched as if trying to get in.
Several days and nights thus wore away, the monotony all the time becoming greater, until at last it became almost unendurable. Some days I would go without any fire at all, and eat raw frozen meat and melt snow in my mouth for water. I became almost convinced that Harrington had been caught in the storm and had been buried under the snow, or was lost. Many a time during that dreary period of uncertainty, I made up my mind that if I ever got out of that place alive, I would abandon the plains and the life of a trapper forever. I had nearly given up all hopes of leaving the dugout alive.
It was on the twenty-ninth day, while I was lying thus despondently thinking and wondering, that I heard the cheerful sound of Harrington’s voice as he came slowly up the creek, yelling, “whoa! haw!” to his cattle. A criminal on the scaffold, with the noose around his neck, the trap about to be sprung, and receiving a pardon just at the last moment, thus giving him a new lease of life, could not have been more grateful than I was at that time. It was useless for me to try to force the door open, as the snow had completely blockaded it, and I therefore anxiously awaited Harrington’s arrival.
“Hello! Billy!” he sang out in a loud voice as he came up, he evidently being uncertain as to my being alive.
“All right, Dave,” was my reply.
“Well, old boy, you’re alive, are you?” said he.
“Yes; and that’s about all. I’ve had a tough siege of it since you’ve been away, and I came pretty nearly passing in my chips. I began to think you never would get here, as I was afraid you had been snowed under,” said I.
He soon cleared away the snow from the entrance, and opening the door he came in.

===Chinese===

我心知肚明这场骇人暴雪必定会严重延误哈灵顿返程的脚步；我深恐他已在暴风雪中不幸冻毙横死，或者在途中遭遇了其他横祸意外。说不定半道上迎头撞上一帮游荡的印第安凶徒，早已命丧黄泉。
种种绝望可怕的念头在我脑海中挥之不去，我必须坦承，当时的处境可谓绝望至极。终于熬到了第20天——原本哈灵顿应当如约班师的日子——我从清晨数着指头挨到深夜，然而这一天在死寂中悄然溜走，荒原上毫无半点哈灵顿的身影。成群结队的恶狼用凄厉渗人的嚎叫撕裂着漫漫长夜；它们黑压压聚集在地窨子四周，在房顶草皮上奔来跑去，拼命用爪子刨抓撕扯着泥土，企图破穴而入。
就这样苦苦熬过了好几个昼夜，令人发疯的死寂孤独与日俱增，直至变得彻底无法承受。有好几天我甚至连火都生不起来，只能嚼食硬邦邦的生冻肉块，含化嘴里的积雪充当饮水解渴。我几乎彻底认定哈灵顿定是遭遇了暴风雪被深埋雪下，或者早已迷途丧命。在漫长无望的迷茫折磨中，我屡次暗自发誓：只要自己能活着走出这个鬼地方，此生必定彻底告别茫茫大平原，永不再干捕兽猎人这一行。对于能否活着爬出这间地窨子，我几乎彻底丧失了最后一丝希望。
到了第29天，正当我直挺挺躺在穴中陷入绝望的胡思乱想与哀叹之际，隐隐约约听见远处溪流下游传来了哈灵顿那声熟悉欢畅的呼号声，正一路大声呵斥着他的挽牛：“喔！嗬！”对于一个被押上绞刑架、绳套已然套上脖颈、踏板即将抽掉的死囚，在最后一秒钟突然接获赦免令重获新生时的感恩与狂喜，也绝不会超越我那一瞬间的心境。由于大雪将房门死死堵死，我想破门而出纯属徒劳，只能焦灼万分地屏息静候哈灵顿走近。
“喂！比利！”他策马靠近时扯开破锣嗓子大声呼喊，显然连他自己也拿不准我究竟是死是活。
“我还活着，戴夫！”我扯起嗓门拼尽全力回应道。
“老天爷啊老弟，你竟然真活着！”他惊喜地叫道。
“是啊；但也只剩下半条命了。你走后我可算是历经了一场九死一生的苦难浩劫，差一丁点儿就把小命给彻底交待了。我还以为你这辈子再也到不了啦，生怕你早就被深埋在暴雪底下了。”我说。
他风卷残云般迅速清除了门前的积雪，一把推开房门疾步冲了进来。

===Original===

I don’t think there ever was a more welcome visitor than he was. I remember that I was so glad to see him that I put my arms around his neck and hugged him for five minutes; never shall I forget faithful Dave Harrington.
“Well, Billy, my boy, I hardly expected to see you alive again,” said Harrington, as soon as I had given him an opportunity to draw his breath; “I had a terrible trip of it, and I didn’t think I ever would get through. I was caught in the snowstorm, and was laid up for three days. The cattle wandered away, and I came within an ace of losing them altogether. When I got started again the snow was so deep that it prevented me from making much headway. But as I had left you here I was bound to come through, or die in the attempt.”
Again I flung my arms around Dave’s neck and gave him a hug that would have done honor to a grizzly bear. My gratitude was thus much more forcibly expressed than it could have been by words. Harrington understood this, and seemed to appreciate it. The tears of joy rolled down my cheeks, and it was impossible for me to restrain them. When my life had been threatened by the Indians I had not felt half so miserable as when I lay in the dugout thinking I was destined to die a slow death by starvation and cold. The Indians would have made short work of it, and would have given me little or no time to think of my fate.
I questioned Harrington as to his trip, and learned all the details. He had passed through hardships which but few men could have endured. Noble fellow, that he was. He had risked his own life to save mine.
After he had finished his story, every word of which I had listened to with eager interest, I related to him my own experiences, in which he became no less interested. He expressed great astonishment that the Indians had not killed me, and he considered it one of the luckiest and most remarkable escapes he had ever heard of. It amused me, however, to see him get very angry when I told him that they had taken my gun and pistol and had used up our provisions. “But never mind, Billy,” said he, “we can stand it till the snow goes off, which will not be long, and then we will pull our wagon back to the settlements.”
A few days afterwards Harrington gathered up our traps, and cleaned the snow out of the wagon.

===Chinese===

我想世间再没有比他更受欢迎的尊贵来客了。我至今仍清楚地记得自己当时是何等狂喜，我一把搂住他的脖子，紧紧抱住他整整痛哭了5分钟；忠诚赤胆的戴夫·哈灵顿，我此生永远难以忘怀。
“哎呀比利，我的好兄弟，我压根就没指望还能见到你活人，”等我终于松开手让他能喘上一口气时，哈灵顿心有余悸地叹道，“这一路上简直是一场炼狱般的苦难噩梦，我好几次都觉得自己绝不可能活着闯过来了。我在半道撞上暴风雪，被活活困了整整三天。挽牛也走丢了，险些就彻底跟丢找不回来。好不容易重新启程，积雪又深得寸步难行。但一想到我把你一个人孤零零扔在这荒郊野岭，我就横下一条心：哪怕豁出这条命，我也必须拼死赶过来！”
我再次扑上去搂住戴夫的脖颈，给了他一个足以让灰熊都自愧不如的死死熊抱。这种无声的拥抱比世间任何华丽的言语都更深切有力地表达了我的再生感激之情。哈灵顿完全心领神会，深感欣慰与温暖。欣喜若狂的热泪止不住顺着我的面颊夺眶而出，任凭我怎么努力也无法遏止。此前当印第安勇士以屠刀逼近我咽喉时，我心底的凄凉绝望尚不及独自瘫卧地穴、眼睁睁看着自己注定在饥寒交迫中被慢慢折磨致死之万一。印第安人杀人不过头点地，手起刀落瞬间了结，几乎不给人留下胡思乱想痛苦命运的闲工夫。
我急切地向哈灵顿打听他一路上的遭遇，获悉了所有惊心动魄的细节。他所承受克服的严酷险阻，世间鲜有人能咬牙硬撑下来。真是个顶天立地、重情重义的高尚汉子！为了拯救我的性命，他真正做到了舍生忘死。
听完他那段令我屏息凝神的惊险叙述后，我也将自己这段时间经历的九死一生向他倾诉了一番，同样听得他目瞪口呆。得知印第安人竟奇迹般网开一面饶我不死，他惊叹不已，直呼这是他平生听说过的最侥幸、最不可思议的死里逃生奇迹。然而，当我向他提起印第安人夺走了我的枪支弹药、吃光了我们的存粮时，看着他气得咬牙切齿暴跳如雷的模样，我不禁觉得颇为滑稽好笑。“不过没关系，比利，”他安慰我道，“咱们再熬上几天等积雪融化，要不了多久大雪就会化掉，到那时咱们就能拉着大车重返文明定居点了。”
几天之后，哈灵顿收拢了全部捕兽铁夹，并清理干净了大车里的积雪。

===Original===

Covering it with the sheet which we had used in the dugout, he made a comfortable bed inside, and helped me into it. We had been quite successful in trapping, having caught three hundred beavers and one hundred otters, the skins of which Harrington loaded on the wagon. We then pulled out for the settlements, making good headway, as the snow had nearly disappeared, having been blown or melted away, so that we had no difficulty in finding a road. On the eighth day out we came to a farmer’s house, or ranch, on the Republican River, where we stopped and rested for two days, and then went on to the ranch where Harrington had obtained the yoke of cattle. We gave the owner of the team twenty-five beaver skins, equal to $60, for the use of the cattle, and he let us have them until we reached Junction City, sending his boy with us to bring them back.
At Junction City we sold our wagon and furs and went with a government mule train to Leavenworth﻿—arriving there in March, 1860. I was just able to get around on crutches when I got into Leavenworth, and it was several months after that before I entirely recovered the use of my leg.
During the winter I had often talked to Harrington about my mother and sisters, and had invited him to go home with me in the spring. I now renewed the invitation, which he accepted, and accompanied me home. When I related to mother my adventures and told her how Harrington had saved my life, she thanked him again and again. I never saw a more grateful woman than she was. She asked him to always make his home with us, as she never could reward him sufficiently for what he had done for her darling boy, as she called me. Harrington concluded to remain with us through the summer and farm mother’s land. But alas! the uncertainty of life. The coming of death when least expected was strikingly illustrated in his case. During the latter part of April he went to a nursery for some trees, and while coming home late at night he caught a severe cold and was taken seriously sick, with lung fever. Mother did everything in her power for him. She could not have done more had he been her own son, but notwithstanding her motherly care and attention, and the skill of a physician from Leavenworth, he rapidly grew worse.

===Chinese===

他在车厢里铺上我们此前在地窨子里使用的床单，精心布置了一张舒适温暖的卧榻，随后搀扶着我爬进了车厢。我们在捕兽营生上战果颇丰，总共捕获了300只河狸与100只水獭，哈灵顿将这些价值连城的皮毛整整齐齐码放在大车上。随后我们启程奔赴定居点，行军进展异常顺遂，因为暴风雪已基本消散，大雪或被狂风吹散，或在暖阳下融化殆尽，使我们毫不费力便能辨认出行车道路。行进至第8天，我们在里帕布利肯河畔遇到了一户农庄牧场，我们在那里落脚休整了整整两天，随后继续驱车前往哈灵顿当初租借挽牛的那座牧场。我们送给牛主人25张上等河狸皮——相当于60美元的重金——作为租借挽牛的酬劳，牛主人慷慨地准许我们一直使用牛队直到章克申城，并打发自家儿子随行照看以便事后将牲口牵回。
在章克申城，我们变卖了大车与全部珍贵毛皮，随后搭乘联邦政府的运骡车队直奔莱文沃思，于1860年3月顺利抵运该地。刚进莱文沃思时，我仅能拄着双拐艰难挪步，足足休养了好几个月，我那条断腿才彻底恢复了行走功能。
在那个严冬里，我常常在穴中向哈灵顿深情谈起我的母亲与姐妹们，并曾盛情邀请他在开春后随我一同回家做客。此刻我再次重申了这番邀请，他欣然接受，陪同我一道回到了家中。当我向母亲娓娓道来这一路上的惊险遭遇、讲述哈灵顿如何舍命挽救我的性命时，母亲拉着他的手千恩万谢、热泪盈眶。我生平从未见过哪位女性表现出如此刻骨铭心的感激之情。她恳请哈灵顿从此永远把我们家当作他自己的家，因为她自认无论如何报答，也偿还不清他对她“心肝宝贝儿子”的救命大恩。哈灵顿决定整个夏季都留下来与我们同住，替母亲耕种田地。然而呜呼哀哉！天有不测风云，人有旦夕祸福，死亡往往在人们最猝不及防时悄然降临，哈灵顿的不幸遭遇便是这一无常宿命的最残酷写照。在4月下旬的一天，他前往一处苗圃采办树苗，深夜冒寒赶路回家途中偶感严重风寒，随即突发恶疾，患上了极其凶险的急性肺炎。母亲倾尽全副心力悉心照料侍奉他；即便对待自己的亲生骨肉，母亲所能做到的也绝不可能超越于此；然而，尽管母亲给予了他无微不至的母爱照料，尽管我们专门从莱文沃思请来了医术最高超的名医诊治，他的病情依然不可逆转地急转直下。

===Original===

It seemed hard, indeed, to think that a great strong man like Harrington, who had braved the storms, and endured the other hardships of the plains all winter long, should, during the warm and beautiful days of spring, when surrounded by friends and the comforts of a good home, be fatally stricken down. But such was his fate. He died one week from the day on which he was taken sick. We all mourned his loss as we would that of a loved son or brother, as he was one of the truest, bravest, and best of friends. Amid sorrow and tears we laid him away to rest in a picturesque spot on Pilot Knob. His death cast a gloom over our household, and it was a long time before it was entirely dispelled. I felt very lonely without Harrington, and I soon wished for a change of scene again.

===Chinese===

眼睁睁看着像哈灵顿这样一位体魄如铁塔般魁梧健壮的伟岸汉子，一位能整冬傲视呼啸暴风雪、硬扛茫茫大草原一切严酷折磨的钢铁男儿，竟然在春光明媚、百花盛开的温馨时节，在挚友的悉心呵护与温馨舒适的家园环抱中，被这等无常死神残忍击倒夺去生命，这命运委实残酷得令人心碎。然而这就是他无法逃脱的宿命。从他突发高烧病倒之日算起，仅仅过了一周，他便溘然长逝。我们全家人放声痛哭为他送终，悲痛之情绝不亚于痛失挚爱的骨肉至亲或手足兄弟，因为他是这世上最忠诚、最勇敢、最无可挑剔的生死挚友。在撕心裂肺的悲痛与泪水中，我们将他安葬在领航峰（Pilot Knob）上一处风景如画的宁静山坡上。他的离世给我们的家庭笼罩上了一层厚重的阴霾，久久未曾散去。失去了哈灵顿的身影，我感到无尽的孤独与落寞，不久我又开始深深渴望重返旷野，渴望寻找全新的生活天地。

## XXIII Administering Justice / 第二十三章 伸张正义
===Original===

On returning to Fort McPherson we found that Brevet Major General W. H. Emory, Colonel of the Fifth Cavalry, and Brevet Brigadier General Thomas Duncan, Lieutenant Colonel of the regiment, had arrived there during our absence. General Emory had been appointed to the command of the District of the Republican, with headquarters at Fort McPherson. As the command had been continually in the field, it was generally thought that we were to have a long rest; and it looked as if this post was to be my home and headquarters for some time to come. I accordingly sent to St. Louis for my wife and daughter to join me there. General Emory promised to build a house for me, but before the building was completed my family arrived.
During the fall of 1869 there were two or three scouting expeditions sent out; but nothing of very great importance was accomplished by them. I found Fort McPherson to be a lively and pleasant post to be stationed at, especially as there was plenty of game in the vicinity, and within a day’s ride there were large herds of deer, antelope and elk.
During the winter of 1869﻿–﻿70 I spent a great deal of time in pursuit of game, and during the season we had two hunting parties of Englishmen there; one party being that of Mr. Flynn, and the other that of George Boyd Houghton, of London﻿—the well known caricaturist. Among their amusements were several horse races, which I arranged, and in which Tall Bull and Powder Face were invariably the winners. Tall Bull by this time had such a reputation as a running horse, that it was difficult to make a race for him. I remember one however, in which he ran against a horse in Captain Spaulding’s Company of the Second Cavalry.
This race was rather a novel affair. I had made a bet that Tall Bull would beat the Second Cavalry horse around a one mile track, and, during the time that he was running, I would jump off and on the horse eight times. I rode the horse bareback; seized his mane with my left hand, rested my right on his withers, and while he was going at full speed, I jumped to the ground, and sprang again upon his back, eight times in succession. Such feats I had seen performed in the circus and I had practiced considerably at it with Tall Bull, so that I was certain of winning the race in the manner agreed upon.

===Chinese===

返回麦克弗森堡（Fort McPherson）后，我们发现第五骑兵团团长、名誉少将W·H·埃默里（Brevet Major General W. H. Emory）与该团副团长、名誉准将托马斯·邓肯（Brevet Brigadier General Thomas Duncan）已在我们出征期间抵运该哨所。埃默里将军被任命为里帕布利肯军区（District of the Republican）司令官，指挥部设在麦克弗森堡。由于部队此前常年征战在野外前线，全军上下普遍认为大家即将迎来一段漫长的休整期；看起来这座哨所将在今后相当长一段岁月里成为我的常驻大本营与安乐窝。我随即打发人前往圣路易斯，接我的妻子与女儿前来要塞与我团聚。埃默里将军承诺亲自为我修建一座宅邸，但在房屋竣工落成之前，我的家眷便已风尘仆仆抵运要塞。
1869年秋，要塞派出过两三支前哨侦察支队，但均未建树什么重大战果。我发现驻防麦克弗森堡是一段极其惬意欢快的岁月，尤其是哨所周边野生猎物异常充裕，只消策马骑行一天的路程，便能撞见大群的鹿、叉角羚与麋鹿。
1869至1870年冬，我把大把大把的时光倾注在狩猎荒原上。那个季节里，有两批英国贵族狩猎旅行团远道而来造访此地；一批由弗林先生（Mr. Flynn）带队，另一批则是伦敦赫赫有名的讽刺漫画家乔治·博伊德·霍顿（George Boyd Houghton）一行。在他们的诸多消遣娱乐项目中，包括好几场由我亲自组织的赛马盛会，在这些赛马中，我的名驹“高牛”（Tall Bull）与“扑面火药”（Powder Face）毫无悬念地场场夺冠。此时此刻，高牛作为平原飞毛腿的神驹盛名早已威震四方，以至于很难再给它物色到旗鼓相当的对手。但我仍清晰记得其中一场精彩角逐，当时它的对手是第二骑兵团斯波尔丁上尉（Captain Spaulding）连里的一匹顶尖快马。
这场赛马相当新颖刺激。我立下赌注：高牛不仅能在一英里环形跑道上跑赢第二骑兵团的战马，而且在它全速狂飙奔驰期间，我还要在马背上连续翻上跃下整整八次。我光着马背跨骑其上，左手死死抓住马鬃，右手紧按在马肩隆上；就在战马疾驰如电、全速爆发之际，我纵身跃向地面，双脚点地后腾空弹起重新跃上马背，如此一连利落地完成了整整八次。这等绝活我过去只在马戏团特技表演里见过，平日里我骑着高牛苦练过许多次，因此成竹在胸，深信自己必定能按约定规矩一举夺魁。

===Original===

Early one morning, in the spring of 1870, the Indians, who had approached during the night, stole some twenty-one head of horses from Mr. John Burke﻿—a Government contractor﻿—Ben Gallagher and Jack Waite. They also ran off some horses from the post; among the number being my pony Powder Face. The commandant at once ordered out Lieutenant Thomas with Company I of the Fifth Cavalry, and directed me to accompany them as trailer. We discovered the trail after some little difficulty, as the Indians were continually trying to hide it, and followed it sixty miles, when darkness set in.
We were now within about four miles of Red Willow Creek and I felt confident the Indians would camp that night in that vicinity. Advising Lieutenant Thomas to halt his company and “lay low” I proceeded on to the creek, where, moving around cautiously, I suddenly discovered horses feeding in a bend of the stream on the opposite side. I hurried back to the troops with the information, and Lieutenant Thomas moved his company to the bank of the creek, with the intention of remaining there until daylight, and then, if possible, surprise the Indians.
Just at break of day we mounted our horses, and after riding a short distance we ascended a slight elevation, when, not over one hundred yards distant, we looked down into the Indian camp. The Indians, preparing to make an early start, had driven up their horses and were in the act of mounting, when they saw us charging down upon them. In a moment they sprang upon their ponies and dashed away. Had it not been for the creek, which lay between us and them, we would have got them before they could have mounted their horses; but as it was rather miry, we were unexpectedly delayed. The Indians fired some shots at us while we were crossing, but as soon as we got across we went for them in hot pursuit. A few of the redskins had not had time to mount and had started on foot down the creek toward the brush. One of these was killed.
A number of our soldiers, who had been detailed before the charge to gather up any of the Indian horses that would be stampeded, succeeded in capturing thirty-two. I hurriedly looked over them to see if Powder Face was among them; but he was not there. Starting in pursuit of the fugitives I finally espied an Indian mounted on my favorite, dashing away and leading all the others.

===Chinese===

1870年春的一个清晨，趁着夜色掩护潜行摸近的一伙印第安人，盗走了军方承包商约翰·伯克先生（Mr. John Burke）、本·加拉格尔（Ben Gallagher）与杰克·韦特（Jack Waite）的大约21匹马。他们还从哨所马厩里顺手卷走了几匹战马，我那匹心爱的小马“扑面火药”赫然也在被盗之列。要塞司令官当即责令托马斯中尉（Lieutenant Thomas）率领第五骑兵团I连紧急出动，并指派我作为随军追踪向导。经过一番周折侦察，我们终于摸清了这伙盗贼的足迹——印第安人沿途费尽心机企图掩盖行踪——我们一路穷追了60英里，夜幕已然深沉降临。
此时我们距离红柳溪（Red Willow Creek）仅约4英里，我笃信印第安人当晚必定会在那一带依水扎营。我建议托马斯中尉叫停全连就地“隐蔽待命”，自己则孤身一人悄悄潜行摸向小溪；在一阵极为谨慎的四下巡查后，我冷不防窥见对岸的一处溪流弯道草地上，大群马匹赫然正在悠闲低头吃草。我疾步飞奔回大队通报军情，托马斯中尉随即率部悄无声息推进至溪岸边，打算隐蔽潜伏到天明拂晓，而后出其不意打敌人一个措手不及。
东方刚泛起鱼肚白，全军便悄然翻身上马；策马推进了一小段距离后，我们悄悄登上一处平缓小高地，在相距不足100码的正下方，印第安人的营寨赫然展现在眼前。印第安人正打算起早拔营赶路，刚刚把马群赶拢过来，正准备翻身上马；冷不防望见官兵如猛虎下山般俯冲杀下。刹那间，他们仓皇跳上马背四散狂奔。若非我们与敌营之间横亘着一条泥泞难行的溪流，我们本可以在他们翻身上马前将他们一网打尽；然而溪滩淤泥极深，导致我们的冲锋意料之外地受阻耽搁了片刻。在我们涉水渡溪时，印第安人朝我们胡乱放了几枪，但我们一跃过溪流，便立刻纵马如旋风般展开亡命穷追。有几个印第安凶徒来不及跳上马背，只能徒步连滚带爬顺着小溪向灌木丛里逃窜，其中一人被当场开枪击毙。
此前在发起冲锋前受命负责收拢受惊奔散印第安马匹的几名士兵，成功截获缴获了32匹良驹。我心急如焚地在马群里一通乱找，想看看扑面火药是否混在其中，然而却不见它的踪影。我随即拍马全速追赶溃逃的敌骑，终于远远瞥见一名印第安勇士正跨骑着我那匹心爱的小马狂奔飞驰，一马当先跑在所有逃敌的最前面。

===Original===

We continued the chase for two or three miles, overtaking a couple who were mounted upon one horse. Coming up behind them I fired my rifle, when about thirty feet distant; the ball passed through the backs of both, and they fell headlong to the ground; but I made no stop however just then, for I had my eye on the gentleman who was riding Powder Face. It seemed to be fun for him to run away from us, and run away he did, for the last I saw of him was when he went over a divide, about three miles away. I bade him adieu. On my way back to the Indian camp I stopped and secured the war bonnets and accoutrements of the pair I had killed, and at the same time gently “raised their hair.”
We were feeling rather tired and hungry, as we had started out on the trail thirty-six hours before without a breakfast or taking any food with us; but not a murmur or complaint was heard among the men. In the abandoned Indian camp, however, we found enough dried buffalo meat to give us all a meal, and after remaining there for two hours, to rest our animals, we started on our return to Fort McPherson, where we arrived at night, having traveled 130 miles in two days.
This being the first fight Lieutenant Thomas had ever commanded in, he felt highly elated over his success, and hoped that his name would be mentioned in the special orders for gallantry; sure enough when we returned both he, myself and the whole command received a complimentary mention in a special order. This he certainly deserved for he was a brave, energetic, dashing little officer. The war bonnets which I had captured I turned over to General Carr, with the request that he present them to General Augur, whose daughters were visiting at the post at the time.
Shortly after this, another expedition was organized at Fort McPherson for the Republican River country. It was commanded by General Duncan, who was a jolly, blustering old fellow, and the officers who knew him well, said that we would have a good time, as he was very fond of hunting. He was a good fighter, and one of the officers said that an Indian bullet never could hurt him, as he had been shot in the head with a cannon ball which had not injured him in the least; another said the ball glanced off and killed one of the toughest mules in the army.

===Chinese===

我们纵马穷追了两三英里，追上了合骑在一匹马上的两个印第安人。我策马从后方疾驰迫近，在距离他们约30英尺处举起步枪果断扣动扳机；飞出的铅弹瞬间洞穿了两个人的后背，二人一头栽下马鞍翻滚在地气绝身亡。但我当时脚下片刻未停，因为我的双眼死死盯着那个骑着扑面火药的狂徒。对我方骑兵的追赶他似乎浑然当作儿戏，策马狂奔而去；我最后望见他时，他已然翻过了大约3英里外的一道山脊分水岭，彻底消失得无影无踪。我只能在心底向我的爱驹依依道别。在折返回印第安营地的路上，我在那两个被我击毙的敌人尸首旁勒马停步，收缴了他们的战冠羽冠与全套行头，顺手极其利落地“揭下了他们的头皮”。
全队将士此刻感到极其困顿饥饿，因为早在36个小时之前我们便在未进早餐、未带半点口粮的情况下轻装急进追击敌踪；然而全军上下没有一个人发出哪怕半句怨言。在敌人丢弃的营寨里，我们搜出了足够的风干野牛肉，供全连饱餐了一顿扎实的野味大餐；在原地休整了两个小时让牲口恢复体力后，我们踏上归途班师返回麦克弗森堡，于当晚顺利进抵要塞——在整整两天的时间里，我们长途奔袭了整整130英里。
由于这是托马斯中尉平生第一次独立指挥战斗，他对所建树的战果感到无比自豪欢欣，期盼着自己的名字能在表彰英勇功绩的特别嘉奖令上榜上有名；果不其然，归队之后，他、我本人以及全连将士均在特别嘉奖令中荣获了通报表彰。这份殊荣托马斯中尉当之无愧，因为他是一位骁勇善战、雷厉风行、英姿勃发的小个子军官。我缴获的那两顶华丽战冠被我呈交给了卡尔将军，恳请他转赠给奥格尔将军（General Augur），当时奥格尔将军的千金小姐们正好在哨所做客探访。
此后不久，麦克弗森堡又针对里帕布利肯河流域组织了一次新的远征军。这支远征军由邓肯将军统帅，他是一个生性豁达开朗、脾气暴躁粗豪的老牌军官；熟知他的军官们私下说这趟远征大家一定能玩得痛快淋漓，因为老将军平生酷爱打猎。他打起仗来同样骁勇凶悍，一位军官开玩笑说印第安人的子弹根本伤不了他分毫，因为早年一颗实心炮弹曾正中他的脑门，却连他的皮都没擦破半点；另一位军官则打趣说，那颗炮弹其实是被他的硬脑壳给弹开了，当场反弹砸死了全军最壮实结实的一头骡子。

===Original===

The Pawnee scouts who had been mustered out of service, during the winter of 1869 and ’70, were reorganized to accompany this expedition. I was glad of this, as I had become quite attached to one of the officers, Major North, and to many of the Indians. The only white scout we had at the post, besides myself at that time, was John Y. Nelson, whose Indian name was Cha-Sha-Cha-Opoyeo,3 which interpreted means Red-Willow-Fill-the-Pipe. This man is a character in his way; he has a Sioux squaw for a wife, and consequently a half-breed family. John is a good fellow, though as a liar he has but few equals and no superior.
We started out from the post with the regimental band playing the lively air of “The Girl I Left Behind Me.” We made but a short march that day, and camped at night at the head of Fox Creek. Next morning General Duncan sent me word by his orderly that I was to bring up my gun and shoot at a mark with him; but I can assure the reader that I did not feel much like shooting anything except myself, for on the night before, I had returned to Fort McPherson and spent several hours in interviewing the sutler’s store, in Company with Major Brown. I looked around for my gun, and found that I had left it behind. The last I could remember about it was that I had it at the sutler’s store. I informed Major Brown of my loss, who said that I was a nice scout to start out without a gun. I replied that that was not the worst of it, as General Duncan had sent for me to shoot a match with him, and I did not know what to do; for if the old gentleman discovered my predicament, he would very likely severely reprimand me.
“Well, Cody,” said he, “the best you can do is to make some excuse, and then go and borrow a gun from some of the men, and tell the General that you lent yours to some man to go hunting with today. While we are waiting here, I will send back to the post and get your rifle for you.”
I succeeded in obtaining a gun from John Nelson, and then marching up to the General’s headquarters I shot the desired match with him, which resulted in his favor.

===Chinese===

在1869至1870年冬季退役解甲归田的波尼族侦察兵们，为了随同这次远征重新整编集结入伍。对此我由衷感到欣慰，因为我对这支部队的指挥官弗兰克·诺斯少校（Major Frank North）以及营里的许多印第安弟兄怀有深厚的情谊。当时哨所里除了我本人之外，唯一的白人侦察兵名叫约翰·Y·纳尔逊（John Y. Nelson），他的印第安名字叫“查-沙-查-奥波耶奥”（Cha-Sha-Cha-Opoyeo），意译过来便是“红柳装烟袋”。此人是个独具一格的边疆奇人；他娶了一位苏族妇女为妻，生下了一大家子印欧混血儿女。约翰为人豪爽仗义，但论起吹牛撒谎的能耐，世间鲜有人能与他平分秋色，更无人能出其右。
大部队从哨所昂首拔营出发，军乐队在队列前沿高奏着轻快欢畅的名曲《留下的姑娘》（The Girl I Left Behind Me）。当天我们仅仅行军了一小段路程，傍晚时分便在福克斯溪（Fox Creek）源头扎下了营寨。次日清晨，邓肯将军打发勤务兵传话给我，命我扛上我的步枪去跟他比赛射击打靶；然而我敢向各位读者保证，当时我脑子里唯一的念头除了朝自己脑门来一枪之外，压根提不起半点打靶的兴致——因为就在前一天晚上，我偷偷溜回麦克弗森堡，拉上布朗少校在随军杂货铺里推杯换盏痛饮了好几个钟头。我满世界寻找自己的爱枪，惊愕地发现枪竟然被我忘在了脑后！我脑海中关于它的最后记忆，唯独记得昨晚它正靠在杂货铺的柜台边。我把丢枪的糗事告诉了布朗少校，少校哭笑不得地嘲弄说，一个出门打仗连枪都能弄丢的侦察兵可真是天底下一绝。我苦笑着回答说这还不是最糟的，邓肯将军偏偏在此时派人传唤我去同台比武射击，我一时间手足无措不知如何是好；若是让那位脾气暴躁的老将军撞破我的窘境，非得把我狗血淋头严加斥责不可。
“我说科迪啊，”少校出谋划策道，“你眼下上上之策就是编个由头推脱一下，赶快从弟兄们手里借支枪应急，回禀将军说你今天把自己的枪借给战友去打猎了。趁咱们在此地扎营休整的空当，我派个通信兵飞马回哨所替你把步枪取回来。”
我顺利从约翰·纳尔逊手里借到了一支步枪，随后硬着头皮迈步走进将军的大帐，陪他完成了那场靶场较量，最终比赛顺理成章地以将军胜出而圆满收场。

===Original===

This was the first scout the Pawnees had been out on under command of General Duncan, and in stationing his guards around the camp he posted them in a manner entirely different from that of General Carr and Colonel Royal, and he insisted that the different posts should call out the hour of the night thus:
“Post No. 1, nine o’clock, all is well! Post No. 2, nine o’clock, all is well!” etc.
The Pawnees, who had their regular turns at standing upon guard, were ordered to call the hour the same as the white soldiers. This was very difficult for them to do, as there were but few of them who could express themselves in English. Major North explained to them that when the man on post next to them should call out the hour, they must call it also as near like him as possible. It was very amusing to hear them do this. They would try to remember what the other man had said on the post next to them. For instance, a white soldier would call out: “Post No. 1, half-past nine o’clock, all is well!” The Indian standing next to him knew that he was bound to say something in English, and he would sing out something like the following:
“Poss number half pass five cents﻿—go to —﻿—﻿—! I don’t care!”
This system was really so ridiculous and amusing that the General had to give it up, and the order was accordingly countermanded.
Nothing of any great interest occurred on this march, until one day, while proceeding up Prairie Dog Creek,4 Major North and myself went out in advance of the command several miles and killed a number of buffaloes. Night was approaching, and I began to look around for a suitable camping ground for the command. Major North dismounted from his horse and was resting, while I rode down to the stream to see if there was plenty of grass in the vicinity. I found an excellent camping spot, and returning to Major North told him that I would ride over the hill a little way, so that the advance guard could see me. This I did, and when the advance came in sight I dismounted and laid down upon the grass to rest.
Suddenly I heard three or four shots, and in a few moments Major North came dashing up towards me, pursued by eight or ten Indians. I instantly sprang into my saddle, and fired a few shots at the Indians, who by this time had all come in sight, to the number of fifty.

===Chinese===

这是波尼侦察连第一次在邓肯将军麾下执行远征巡察任务。在军营四周布置警戒哨兵时，老将军采用了一套与卡尔将军和罗亚尔上校截然不同的哨戒规矩，他固执地坚持要求各哨位的卫兵必须在漫漫长夜里按钟点大声交替唱哨报更：
“1号哨位，9点整，一切平安！2号哨位，9点整，一切平安！”依此类推。
按规矩轮流承担站岗放哨职责的波尼战士，同样被勒令必须像白人士兵一样按时大声用英语唱哨报时。这对他们而言委实是强人所难，因为他们当中几乎没人能用像样的英语开口说话。诺斯少校只得向他们耐心解释：一旦听到身旁相邻哨位的弟兄大声报更，他们就必须依葫芦画瓢、尽可能模仿对方的发音大声喊出来。听他们唱哨报更的场面着实滑稽可笑到了极点。他们绞尽脑汁拼命试图记住隔壁哨兵刚才嘴里到底吐出了哪些词句。举例而言，白人士兵高呼：“1号哨位，9点半，一切平安！”紧挨着他的印第安哨兵心知肚明自己无论如何也得挤出几句英文，于是扯开嗓门喊出的大体是这副动静：
“1号……半过……5分钱——见你的鬼去吧！老子才不管！”
这套报更规矩闹出的笑话实在过于荒诞滑稽，引得全军前仰后合，最终连老将军自己也忍俊不禁只得彻底作罢，随即正式下令取消了报更令。
此后的行军一路平淡无奇，直到有一天，在沿着草原犬溪溯流而上推进途中，诺斯少校与我策马脱离大部队向前探路数英里，沿途击毙了数头野牛。夜幕渐次降临，我开始在四周物色适宜大军宿营的理想营地。诺斯少校翻身下马在原地休憩，我则策马下到小溪边察看周遭水草是否丰美茂盛。我挑中了一处理想的营址，折返回少校身旁告诉他，我打算策马翻过前沿小山头伫立片刻，好让行军前卫能远远望见我的身影。我依计行事，等大军前锋进入视线后，我便翻身下马，惬意地仰面躺倒在松软的草地上闭目养神。
冷不防，远处猛然炸响了三四声清脆的枪声；片刻之后，只见诺斯少校策马如狂风般朝我疾驰飞奔而来，身后紧追着八九名杀气腾腾的印第安人。我一个鲤鱼打挺翻身跃上马鞍，抬手朝敌人连放几枪；而此时放眼望去，从山梁后涌现出的印第安狂徒已多达50余人。

===Original===

We turned our horses and ran, the bullets flying after us thick and fast﻿—my whip being shot from my hand and daylight being put through the crown of my hat. We were in close quarters, when suddenly Lieutenant Valkmar came galloping up to our relief with several soldiers, and the Indians seeing them whirled and retreated. As soon as Major North got in sight of his Pawnees, he began riding in a circle. This was a sign to them that there were hostile Indians in front, and in a moment the Pawnees broke ranks pell-mell and, with Major North at their head, started for the flying warriors. The rest of the command pushed rapidly forward also, and chased the enemy for three or four miles, killing three of them.
But this was a wrong move on our part, as their village was on Prairie Dog Creek, while they led us in a different direction; one Indian only kept straight on up the creek﻿—a messenger to the village. Some of the command, who had followed him, stirred up the village and accelerated its departure. We finally got back to the main force, and then learned that we had made a great mistake. Now commenced another stern chase.
The second day that we had been following these Indians we came upon an old squaw, whom they had left on the prairie to die. Her people had built for her a little shade or lodge, and had given her some provisions, sufficient to last her on her trip to the Happy Hunting grounds. This the Indians often do when pursued by an enemy, and one of their number becomes too old and feeble to travel any longer. This squaw was recognized by John Nelson who said that she was a relative of his wife. From her we learned that the flying Indians were known as Pawnee Killer’s band, and that they had lately killed Buck’s surveying party, consisting of eight or nine men; the massacre having occurred a few days before on Beaver Creek. We knew that they had had a fight with surveyors, as we found quite a number of surveying instruments, which had been left in the abandoned camp. We drove these Indians across the Platte River and then returned to Fort McPherson, bringing the old squaw with us, from there she was sent to the Spotted Tail Agency.
During my absence, my wife had given birth to a son, and he was several weeks old when I returned.

===Chinese===

我们猛拉缰绳调转马头亡命飞奔，呼啸的铅弹如暴雨般密密麻麻从身后倾泻而过——我手中的马鞭被一枪齐齐打飞，头上的毡帽顶更是被子弹当场贯穿打出了一个大窟窿。眼看我们就要陷入绝境，千钧一发之际，瓦尔克马尔中尉（Lieutenant Valkmar）率领数名骑兵策马如旋风般赶来驰援；印第安人一见官兵大队人马杀到，急忙调转马头仓皇撤退。诺斯少校一俟进入波尼族战士的视线，便立刻勒马在原地打起转来。这是他们之间通用的军情暗号，意为前沿遭遇敌对部落；刹那间，波尼勇士们阵型大乱如潮水般一拥而上，在诺斯少校的引领下一马当先直扑溃退的敌方勇士。大部队主力随即全速压上追击，将敌人穷追猛打了三四英里，当场击毙了三名敌兵。
然而事后证明，我们这步棋完全中了调虎离山之计：敌人的村落大营原本就扎在草原犬溪下游，而这群敌人却故意将我们引向了截然相反的方向；唯独一名印第安骑手笔直沿着溪流狂奔而去——那正是赶往敌营通风报信的传令兵。我军部分跟在后头的先头部队虽曾追赶过他，却打草惊蛇惊动了敌营，促使他们仓皇拔营开溜。等我们最终与主力汇合时，才扼腕叹息意识到自己犯下了不可挽回的弥天大错。一场漫长艰苦的强行长途追逐战就此拉开序幕。
在追踪这伙逃敌的第二天，我们在荒原草地上偶遇了一名被族人遗弃等死的老妇人。她的族人为她搭了一处简陋的遮阳棚棚舍，留给了她少许糊口的口粮，仅够维持她在前往“快乐狩猎场”（Happy Hunting grounds，印第安天堂）的黄泉路上果腹。印第安人在遭到大军穷追不舍、队伍中有人年老体衰无法继续长途跋涉时，往往便会采用这等残忍习俗。约翰·纳尔逊一眼认出了这位老妇，说她是自己妻子的远房亲戚。从她口中我们得知，这伙逃遁的印第安人正是臭名昭著的“波尼杀手”（Pawnee Killer）匪帮；不久之前他们刚刚血洗了巴克（Buck）的测绘测量队，残忍屠杀了八九名测绘员；这场惨案就发生在数天前比弗溪（Beaver Creek）沿岸。我们心知他们确实刚与测绘队血战过，因为我们在他们丢弃的旧营地里搜出了大批被遗弃的测绘仪器。我们一路将这帮歹徒逐过了普拉特河，随后押解着这名印第安老妇班师返回麦克弗森堡；从那里，老妇被遣送到了斑尾（Spotted Tail）印第安保留区代管所。
在我随军出征期间，我的妻子为我诞下了一个白白胖胖的儿子；等我凯旋归来时，小家伙已经有好几周大了。

===Original===

No name had yet been given him and I selected that of Elmo Judson, in honor of Ned Buntline; but this the officers and scouts objected to. Major Brown proposed that we should call him Kit Carson, and it was finally settled that that should be his name.
During the summer we made one or two more scouts and had a few skirmishes with the Indians: but nothing of any great importance transpired. In the fall of 1870, while I was a witness in a court martial at Fort D. A. Russell I woke up one morning and found that I was dead broke;﻿—this is not an unusual occurrence to a frontiersman, or an author I may add, especially when he is endeavoring to kill time﻿—to raise necessary funds I sold my race horse Tall Bull to Lieutenant Mason, who had long wanted him.
In the winter of 1870 and 1871 I first met George Watts Garland, an English gentleman, and a great hunter, whom I had the pleasure of guiding on several hunts and with whom I spent some weeks. During the winter I also took several parties out on the Loupe River country, hunting and trapping. Although I was still chief of scouts I did not have much to do, as the Indians were comparatively quiet, thus giving me plenty of time for sporting.
In the spring of 1871 several short scouting expeditions were sent out from Fort McPherson, but all with minor results.
About this time General Emory was considerably annoyed by petty offenses committed in the vicinity of the post, and as there was no justice of the peace in the neighborhood, he was anxious to have such an officer there to attend to the civilians; one day he remarked to me that I would make an excellent justice.
“General, you compliment me rather too highly, for I don’t know any more about law than a government mule does about bookkeeping,” said I.
“That doesn’t make any difference,” said he, “for I know that you will make a good ’Squire.” He accordingly had the county commissioners appoint me to the office of justice of the peace, and I soon received my commission.
One morning a man came rushing up to my house and stated that he wanted to get out a writ of replevin, to recover possession of a horse which a stranger was taking out of the country. I had no blank forms, and had not yet received the statutes of Nebraska to copy from, so I asked the man:
“Where is the fellow who has got your horse?”
“He is going up the road, and is about two miles away,” replied he.

===Chinese===

当时孩子尚未正式取名，为了向老友内德·邦特莱恩（Ned Buntline）致敬，我最初为他挑选的名字叫埃尔莫·贾德森（Elmo Judson）；然而要塞里的军官与侦察兵同袍们却纷纷表示反对。布朗少校提议咱们何不干脆叫他“基特·卡森”（Kit Carson），最终全票通过，孩子的名字就此一锤定音。
整个盛夏里，我们又执行了一两次巡逻侦察任务，与印第安人打过几场小规模的前哨遭遇战；但均未发生什么足以载入史册的重大战事。1870年秋，在D·A·拉塞尔堡（Fort D. A. Russell）的一场军事法庭军事审判中出庭担任证人期间，一天清晨醒来我惊愕地发现自己囊空如洗、彻彻底底破了产——对于一个边疆拓荒汉子，或者我顺便添上一句，对于一位成天变着法子消磨时光的作家而言，这等窘境可谓司空见惯——为了筹措燃眉之急的盘缠，我忍痛将我的冠军赛马高牛卖给了梅森中尉（Lieutenant Mason），此人长久以来一直对这匹宝马垂涎三尺。
在1870至1871年冬，我初次结识了乔治·沃茨·加兰（George Watts Garland）——一位来自英国的绅士，也是一位卓越出众的狂热猎手。我荣幸地担任向导陪同他在荒原上进行了多次狩猎，与他朝夕相处了数周之久。那个冬季里，我还多次带领各路队伍深入卢普河流域（Loupe River）开展狩猎与捕兽。尽管我依然头顶侦察兵总指挥的头衔，但由于印第安各部局势相对风平浪静，我的正经差事并不多，这使我有充裕的大把闲暇沉醉于狩猎消遣。
1871年春，麦克弗森堡先后派出了几支小规模的巡逻侦察小队，但斩获均微不足道。
大约就在此时，要塞周边平民犯下的零星治安违法琐事令埃默里将军不胜其烦；鉴于邻近辖区一直没有设立正式的治安法官（justice of the peace），将军急于推选一位德高望重的长官来专司处置平民纷争；一天他打趣地对我说，凭我的声望绝对能胜任一位铁面无私的优秀法官。
“将军，您这顶高帽子戴得未免太过了，”我坦率地笑道，“论起法条律例，我懂的门道跟军需处拉大车的骡子对簿记账目的了解没什么两样。”
“那根本无关紧要，”他说，“凭你一身正气，我深信你定能成为一名深得民心的好乡绅法官。”于是他出面协调县政委员公署，正式任命我为本地治安法官；不久，委任公文便郑重颁发到了我的手中。
一天早晨，一名汉子气喘吁吁冲进我的家门，声称要申请开具一张追索被盗动产令状（writ of replevin），从一个正准备把马运出境的外乡人手里强行追回属于自己的战马。我手头既没有现成的判决令状空白公文表格，也尚未收到配发的《内布拉斯加州成文法典》以供誊抄参考，于是我直接质问来人：
“拐带你马匹的那个家伙人在哪儿？”
“他正沿着大路往前赶，距此大概就两英里路。”那人焦急地回答道。

===Original===

“Very well,” said I, “I will get the writ ready in a minute or two.”
I saddled up my horse, and then taking my old reliable gun, “Lucretia,” I said to the man: “That’s the best writ of replevin that I can think of; come along, and we’ll get that horse, or know the reason why.”
We soon overtook the stranger who was driving a herd of horses, and as we came up to him, I said:
“Hello, sir; I am an officer, and have an attachment for that horse,” and at the same time I pointed out the animal.
“Well, sir, what are you going to do about it?” he inquired.
“I propose to take you and the horse back to the post,” said I.
“You can take the horse,” said he, “but I haven’t the time to return with you.”
“You’ll have to take the time, or pay the costs here and now,” said I.
“How much are the costs?”
“Twenty dollars.”
“Here’s your money,” said he, as he handed me the greenbacks.
I then gave him a little friendly advice, and told him that he was released from custody. He went on his way a wiser and a poorer man, while the owner of the horse and myself returned to the fort. I pocketed the twenty dollars, of course. Some people might think it was not a square way of doing business, but I didn’t know any better just then. I had several little cases of this kind, and I became better posted on law in the course of time, being assisted by Lieutenant Burr Reilly, of the Fifth Cavalry, who had been educated for a lawyer.
One evening I was called upon to perform a marriage ceremony. The bridegroom was one of the sergeants of the post. I had “braced up” for the occasion by imbibing rather freely of stimulants, and when I arrived at the house, with a copy of the Statutes of Nebraska, which I had recently received, I felt somewhat confused. Whether my bewilderment was owing to the importance of the occasion and the large assembly, or to the effect of Louis Woodin’s “tanglefoot,” I cannot now distinctly remember﻿—but my suspicions have always been that it was due to the latter cause. I looked carefully through the statutes to find the marriage ceremony, but my efforts were unsuccessful. Finally the time came for the knot to be tied. I told the couple to stand up, and then I said to the bridegroom:
“Do you take this woman to be your lawful wedded wife, to support and love her through life?”
“I do,” was the reply.

===Chinese===

“那好办，”我说，“两分钟之内我就把‘令状’给你备齐。”
我给战马套上鞍鞯，取下我那支百步穿杨的老伙计“卢克雷蒂娅”，回头冲来人招呼道：“这就是我能想出来的天底下最管用的追索动产令状！跟我走，咱们非把那匹马追回来不可，倒要看看谁敢说个不字！”
我们很快策马追上了那个正驱赶着一群马匹的外乡汉子，冲到他跟前，我厉声喝道：
“喂，朋友！我是司法长官，我依法对这匹马实施扣押执行令！”说着，我抬手指了指那匹受害者的马。
“好吧，长官，那您打算拿我怎么办呢？”他有些心虚地反问。
“我打算把你们人赃俱获，押解回哨所法办。”我正色道。
“马你们可以牵走，”他说，“但我手头赶时间，实在没闲工夫陪你们回要塞。”
“你没闲工夫也得腾出工夫，要不然就在此地当场把诉讼罚金开销结清！”我说。
“罚金开销总共多少钱？”
“整整20美元。”
“拿去吧，钱归你们。”说着，他老老实实从兜里掏出几张绿背纸币递到了我手中。
我随即对他进行了一番语重心长的友好训导，宣布正式解除对他的拘留羁押。他心服口服地踏上了归途——虽说变成了一个兜里瘪下去的穷汉，却也变成了一个懂规矩的聪明人；而马主人与我则昂首阔步凯旋返回了要塞。至于那20美元罚金，我自然毫不客气地笑纳揣进了自己腰包。某些认死理的人或许会指责这等断案手法未免过于简单粗暴不够规范，但当时我对法学程序确实一窍不通。后来我又经手审理过几桩类似的小案子；随着时间的推移，在第五骑兵团受过正规法学教育的伯尔·莱利中尉（Lieutenant Burr Reilly）的热心襄助下，我逐渐对正规法律条文变得了如指掌。
一天傍晚，我受邀前去主持一场庄严的婚礼仪式。新郎是哨所里的一名陆军军士。为了在这一重大庄严场合“鼓足底气”，我此前放开海量痛饮了不少烈酒；等我夹着刚刚配发到手的《内布拉斯加州成文法典》晕晕乎乎赶到宅邸现场时，整个人感到脑子一片混沌发懵。至于我当时的晕眩困惑究竟是因为场面过于隆重威严、宾客云集所致，还是路易斯·伍丁（Louis Woodin）私酿的烈性“绊脚水”在暗中作祟，我如今已记不大真切了——但我心底向来怀疑后者的酒劲才是罪魁祸首。我焦头烂额地在法典里来回翻找婚礼主持誓词，却徒劳无功一无所获。最终，喜结连理的吉时已到，再拖延不得。我吩咐新人双双起立肃立，随后清了清嗓子庄重地对新郎宣告：
“你是否愿意迎娶这位女子作为你合法的妻子，用一生去扶持她、爱护她？”
“我愿意。”新郎斩钉截铁地回答。

===Original===

Then addressing myself to the bride, I said, “Do you take this man to be your lawful wedded husband through life, to love, honor and obey him?”
“I do,” was her response.
“Then join hands,” said I to both of them; “I now pronounce you to be man and wife, and whomsoever God and Buffalo Bill have joined together let no man put asunder. May you live long and prosper. Amen.”
This concluded the interesting ceremony, which was followed by the usual festivities on such occasions. I was highly complimented for the elegant and eloquent manner in which I had tied the matrimonial knot.
During the summer of 1871, Professor Marsh, of Yale College, came out to McPherson, with a large party of students to have a hunt and to look for fossils. Professor Marsh had heard of the big bone which had been found by the Pawnees in the Niobrara country, and he intended to look for that as well as other bones. He accordingly secured the services of Major Frank North and the Pawnees as an escort. I was also to accompany the bone-hunters, and would have done so had it not been for the fact that just at that time I was ordered out with a small scouting party to go after some Indians.
The day before the Professor arrived at the fort, I had been out hunting on the north side of the North Platte River, near Pawnee Springs, with several companions, when we were suddenly attacked by Indians, who wounded one of our number, John Weister. We stood the Indians off for a little while, and Weister got even with them by killing one of their party. The Indians, however, outnumbered us, and at last we were forced to make a run for our lives. In this we succeeded, and reached the fort in safety. The General wanted to have the Indians pursued, and said he could not spare me to accompany Professor Marsh.
However, I had the opportunity to make the acquaintance of the eminent Professor, whom I found to be not only a well-posted person but a very entertaining gentleman. He gave me a geological history of the country; told me in what section fossils were to be found; and otherwise entertained me with several scientific yarns, some of which seemed too complicated and too mysterious to be believed by an ordinary man like myself; but it was all clear to him. I rode out with him several miles, as he was starting on his bone-hunting expedition, and I greatly enjoyed the ride.

===Chinese===

随后我转过身来面向新娘，庄严宣告：“你是否愿意接受这位男士作为你合法的丈夫，用一生去敬爱他、尊崇他、顺服他？”
“我愿意。”新娘轻声而坚定地答道。
“那么请二人执手相牵，”我威严地对二位新人宣告，“我现在以法律的名义正式宣布你们结为合法夫妻；凡上帝与野牛比尔（Buffalo Bill）所结合之人，任何人不得将其拆散！愿你们福寿绵长、繁荣昌盛。阿门！”
这场妙趣横生的别致婚礼至此圆满礼成，随后便是此等大喜之日少不了的欢歌笑语与饕餮盛宴。大家对我以如此高贵典雅、雄辩滔滔的神来之笔主持并缔结这一良缘佳偶赞不绝口。
1871年夏，耶鲁大学的马什教授（Professor Marsh）率领一大批青年学子专程来到麦克弗森堡，计划在荒原开展科学考察打猎并搜寻远古化石。马什教授此前听闻波尼人在尼奥布拉拉河流域（Niobrara）发掘出过巨型巨兽骨骼化石，此番专程前来寻觅该古生物化石以及其他骨骼遗迹。他随即出资聘请弗兰克·诺斯少校与波尼侦察连担任武装护卫队。军方原计划也指派我随这支“化石搜寻队”同行，若非就在此时我接到紧急调令、奉命率领一支前哨侦察小队火速出击追剿印第安人，我本定会随教授一同策马远行。
就在教授抵达哨所的前一天，我与几名同袍在北普拉特河北岸的波尼泉（Pawnee Springs）附近狩猎，冷不防遭到了一伙印第安人的突然伏击，我们队里的约翰·韦斯特（John Weister）不幸中弹负伤。我们顽强就地阻击逼退了敌人，韦斯特咬牙还击亲手击毙了一名敌兵扯平了战果。然而印第安人人多势众，最终我们被迫奋力突围亡命飞奔。我们幸运地突围成功，毫发无损地平安撤回了哨所。要塞司令官严令必须对这伙敢于犯境的凶徒展开武装追剿，表示手头兵力吃紧，绝抽调不出我去陪同马什教授寻骨。
不过，我还是有幸结识了这位声名卓著的知名教授，发现他不仅学富五车、博古通今，更是一位极富幽默感与亲和力的谦谦君子。他深入浅出地为我系统讲解了当地的地质变迁史；指点我在哪些具体地层与区域最易发掘出古生物化石；并用许多深奥玄妙的科学典故开阔了我的眼界，其中某些理论对像我这样的边疆大老粗而言未免显得过于晦涩高深、不可思议，然而在他脑海中却条理清晰、如数家珍。在他率队启程踏上寻骨征途之际，我策马陪同他送行了数英里，那段交谈伴行令我感到无比惬意开怀。

===Original===

His party had been provided with Government transportation and his students were all mounted on Government horses.
As we rode along he delivered a scientific lecture, and he convinced me that he knew what he was talking about. I finally bade him goodbye, and returned to the post. While the fossil-hunters were out on their expedition, we had several lively little skirmishes with the Indians. After having been absent some little time Professor Marsh and his party came back with their wagons loaded down with all kinds of bones, and the Professor was in his glory. He had evidently struck a bone-yard, and “gad!”5 wasn’t he happy! But they had failed to find the big bone which the Pawnees had unearthed the year before.

===Chinese===

他的考察队由联邦政府统一配发了大篷车运输工具，随行的大学生们也个个跨骑着军方调拨的精良战马。
策马同行沿途，教授兴致勃勃地发表了一场高深生动的科普演讲，以无可辩驳的真才实学彻底征服了我，让我确信他对自己的学术领域有着登峰造极的深刻造诣。最终我向他拱手道别，策马回师哨所。在化石考察队远行科考期间，我们与印第安人爆发了几次激烈紧张的小规模前哨遭遇战。在外跋涉奔波了一段时间后，马什教授与其考察队凯旋班师，他们的大车上沉甸甸装满了形形色色的古生物骨骼化石，教授整个人沉浸在无比自豪的辉煌喜悦之中。他显然撞上了一处丰饶的远古动物化石大墓场，“老天爷啊！”他当时简直乐得合不拢嘴！不过令人略感遗憾的是，他们终究没能寻获波尼人在前一年掘出的那具名震一时的传奇巨骨。

## VIII Adventures on the Overland Road / 第八章 陆路驿道险遇
===Original===

As the warm days of summer approached I longed for the cool air of the mountains; and to the mountains I determined to go. After engaging a man to take care of the farm, I proceeded to Leavenworth and there met my old wagon master and friend, Lewis Simpson, who was fitting out a train at Atchison and loading it with supplies for the Overland Stage Company, of which Mr. Russell, my old employer, was one of the proprietors. Simpson was going with this train to Fort Laramie and points further west.
“Come along with me, Billy,” said he, “I’ll give you a good layout. I want you with me.”
“I don’t know that I would like to go as far west as that again,” replied I, “but I do want to ride the pony express once more; there’s some life in that.”
“Yes, that’s so; but it will soon shake the life out of you,” said he. “However, if that’s what you’ve got your mind set on, you had better come to Atchison with me and see Mr. Russell, who I’m pretty certain, will give you a situation.”
I replied that I would do that. I then went home and informed mother of my intention, and as her health was very poor I had great difficulty in obtaining her consent. I finally convinced her that as I was of no use on the farm, it would be better and more profitable for me to return to the plains. So after giving her all the money I had earned by trapping, I bade her goodbye and set out for Atchison.
I met Mr. Russell there and asked him for employment as a pony express-rider; he gave me a letter to Mr. Slade, who was then the stage agent for the division extending from Julesburg to Rocky Ridge. Slade had his headquarters at Horseshoe Station, thirty-six miles west of Fort Laramie and I made the trip thither in company with Simpson and his train.
Almost the very first person I saw after dismounting from my horse was Slade. I walked up to him and presented Mr. Russell’s letter, which he hastily opened and read. With a sweeping glance of his eye he took my measure from head to foot, and then said:
“My boy, you are too young for a pony express-rider. It takes men for that business.”
“I rode two months last year on Bill Trotter’s division, sir, and filled the bill then; and I think I am better able to ride now,” said I.
“What! are you the boy that was riding there, and was called the youngest rider on the road?”
“I am the same boy,” I replied, confident that everything was now all right for me.

===Chinese===

随着夏日的炎炎暑气渐浓，我心底开始无比渴望高山之上的清凉空气；我打定主意非奔赴群山不可。在雇下一名长工照料农场后，我动身前往莱文沃思，在那里偶遇了我昔日的大车队工头兼挚友刘易斯·辛普森（Lewis Simpson）。他当时正在艾奇逊（Atchison）装备整肃一支大车队，为陆路长途驿马车公司（Overland Stage Company）运送给养物资，而我的老东家拉塞尔先生（Mr. Russell）恰好正是该公司的合伙老板之一。辛普森正准备率领这支车队前往拉勒米堡以及更西面的据点。
“跟我一道走吧，比利，”他说，“我给你安排个上等差事。我身边正缺你这么个帮手。”
“我倒不太想再跑到那么遥远的西部深处去受罪了，”我答道，“不过我真心还想再去跑小马快递；那营生才叫有奔头、有血性！”
“这话倒不假，可那行当用不了多久就能把你浑身骨头架子给颠散架！”他说，“不过既然你一门心思认准了这条道，不妨跟我一块儿去艾奇逊见见拉塞尔先生，我敢打包票他准能给你谋个快递差事。”
我一口答应了下来。随后我返回家中，向母亲禀报了我的打算；由于她当时体弱多病，我费了九牛二虎之力才求得她的应允。我最终说服了她：既然我留在农场里于农活并无大用，重返大平原闯荡对我而言不仅天地更宽广，而且收益也丰厚得多。因此，在将此前捕兽所得的全部积蓄尽数留给母亲后，我依依不舍地与她吻别，踏上了前往艾奇逊的征程。
我在艾奇逊拜会了拉塞尔先生，向他毛遂自荐申请担任小马快递骑手；他当即给我修书一封，让我呈递给斯莱德先生（Mr. Slade）——此人当时正担任从朱尔斯堡（Julesburg）至落基岭（Rocky Ridge）区间段的驿车总主管。斯莱德的调度大本营设在马蹄铁驿站（Horseshoe Station），位于拉勒米堡以西36英里处，我随同辛普森及其货运大车队一路兼程赶往该地。
翻身跃下马背，我第一眼见到的熟人几乎就是斯莱德本人。我快步走上前去，恭敬地呈上拉塞尔先生的亲笔引荐信，他一把拆开匆匆扫了几眼。随后，他那如鹰隼般锐利的目光从头到脚将我上下狠狠打量了一番，冷冷地说道：
“小子，想当小马快递骑手你未免太嫩了点。干这行得是顶天立地的纯爷们才行。”
“去年我在比尔·特罗特的区间线上跑了整整两个月，长官，当时就完全能独当一面；如今我比那时候更结实，跑起马来更不在话下。”我说。
“什么！原来你就是去年在那条线上跑马、人称全线年纪最小的那个毛头骑手？”
“正是小子我，”我成竹在胸地答道，深知这下大局已定。

===Original===

“I have heard of you before. You are a year or so older now, and I think you can stand it. I’ll give you a trial anyhow and if you weaken you can come back to Horseshoe Station and tend stock.”
That ended our first interview. The next day he assigned me to duty on the road from Red Buttes on the North Platte, to the Three Crossings of the Sweetwater﻿—a distance of seventy-six miles﻿—and I began riding at once. It was a long piece of road, but I was equal to the undertaking; and soon afterwards had an opportunity to exhibit my power of endurance as a pony express rider.
One day when I galloped into Three Crossings, my home station, I found that the rider who was expected to take the trip out on my arrival, had got into a drunken row the night before and had been killed; and that there was no one to fill his place. I did not hesitate for a moment to undertake an extra ride of eighty-five miles to Rocky Ridge, and I arrived at the latter place on time. I then turned back and rode to Red Buttes, my starting place, accomplishing on the round trip a distance of 322 miles.
Slade heard of this feat of mine, and one day as he was passing on a coach he sang out to me, “My boy, you’re a brick, and no mistake. That was a good run you made when you rode your own and Miller’s routes, and I’ll see that you get extra pay for it.”
Slade, although rough at times and always a dangerous character﻿—having killed many a man﻿—was always kind to me. During the two years that I worked for him as pony express-rider and stage-driver, he never spoke an angry word to me.
As I was leaving Horse Creek one day, a party of fifteen Indians “jumped me” in a sand ravine about a mile west of the station. They fired at me repeatedly, but missed their mark. I was mounted on a roan California horse﻿—the fleetest steed I had. Putting spurs and whip to him, and lying flat on his back, I kept straight on for Sweetwater Bridge﻿—eleven miles distant﻿—instead of trying to turn back to Horse Creek. The Indians came on in hot pursuit, but my horse soon got away from them, and ran into the station two miles ahead of them.
